Qulipta (atogepant)

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
This new drug application (NDA) for atogepant was submitted by AbbVie, Inc. (the Applicant).
Atogepant is an oral small molecule calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist,
developed for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine (EM) in adults.
This NDA received a priority review designation because of the Applicant’s use of a Material
Threat Countermeasure Priority Review Voucher. The NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary
review team (Clinical, Clinical Infectious Disease, Clinical Hepatology, Clinical Pharmacology,
Clinical Outcomes Assessment Team, Pharmacology/Toxicology, Chemistry Manufacturing and
Controls, and Statistics) that did not identify any issues that preclude approval. I, the signatory
authority, agree that the benefit-risk assessment supports approval.
Migraine is a primary headache disorder characterized by recurrent headaches that are moderate
to severe, accompanied by various associated symptoms. Chronic migraine (CM) is defined as
15 or more headache days per month, with features of migraine headache on ≥8 days per month.
Patients with 14 or fewer migraine headache days per month are defined as having EM.
The Applicant submitted the results of two adequate and well-controlled trials (Study CGP-MD01 and Study 3101-301-002) to support the efficacy of atogepant for the preventive treatment of
EM in adults. Both trials used a well-validated and clinically meaningful primary efficacy
endpoint: the change from baseline in the mean number of monthly migraine days. Study CGPMD-01 evaluated daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg, and twice-daily doses of 30 mg and
60 mg. The primary endpoint was met in all dosage groups. Given the relatively flat doseresponse relationship observed in this trial, only the daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg
were evaluated in Study 3101-301-002. In that study, all atogepant dosage groups had
significantly greater reductions in the mean number of monthly migraine days than the placebo
group (0.7 to 1.7 fewer monthly migraine days). The treatment effect size on that endpoint is
similar to that of other products approved for the preventive treatment of migraine (EM and/or
CM). In Study 3101-301-002, significant results were also observed for several secondary
endpoints, including reduction in monthly medication use, and patient-reported clinical outcome
assessment tools. The results of the secondary efficacy endpoints in Study CGP-MD-01 all
numerically favored atogepant.
Atogepant has a favorable safety profile. In the controlled trials, there were no deaths and no
serious adverse events with a clear association with atogepant. Common adverse events that
occurred at an incidence of at least 2% of patients on atogepant and greater than placebo were
nausea, constipation, fatigue, and decreased appetite. Safety signals of interest that were
evaluated during the review were vascular ischemic abnormalities, hypertension, and serious
gastrointestinal events, based on known pharmacodynamic effects of CGRP and subsequent
theoretical risks with CGRP antagonism. No serious vascular events or serious gastrointestinal
effects related to the use of atogepant were identified. Hepatotoxicity was also considered a
safety signal of interest as the development programs for two older-generation oral CGRP
antagonists were discontinued because of cases of serious liver injury. The rate of transaminase
3
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

elevations over 3 times the upper limit of normal was similar between atogepant and placebo
(1% for atogepant vs. 1.8% for placebo). However, there were a few cases with elevations over
three times the upper limit of normal assessed as having a probable or possible relationship with
atogepant treatment. All cases resolved after discontinuation of atogepant. Routine monitoring of
liver enzymes is not recommended. The risks of atogepant can be mitigated through labeling and
pharmacovigilance.
This application triggers the Pediatric Research Equity Act; therefore, pediatric postmarketing
requirements to study safety and efficacy of this product for the preventive treatment of EM in
patients age 6 to less than 18 years of age will be issued. In addition, postmarketing requirements
for a pregnancy registry study, a pregnancy outcomes study, a nonclinical dose-range finding
study, and a 2-year nonclinical carcinogenicity study will be issued.
Based on the review of all available efficacy and safety data for atogepant, the benefits outweigh
the risks for the preventive treatment of EM in adults. This product provides for a second oral
CGRP receptor antagonist for this indication.
For detailed information supporting the basis for the benefit-risk assessment, please refer to the
details in this integrated assessment document.

Conclusions and Reasons

Migraine is a primary headache disorder characterized by recurrent headaches that Episodic migraine is a common and
are moderate to severe, accompanied by associated symptoms of nausea,
debilitating disease that can significantly
vomiting, and light and/or sound sensitivity. The typical headache of migraine is
impact the quality of life.
throbbing, unilateral, and aggravated by motion, but bilateral and nonthrobbing
headaches are also commonly reported. Migraine attacks typically last 4 to 72
hours when untreated. About one third of people with migraine experience transient
neurological symptoms before and/or during an attack, referred to as migraine aura.
Migraine is more prevalent in females than males and peaks in the fourth decade of
life.
Migraine patients can be categorized as having either episodic or chronic migraine.
Episodic migraine refers to patients with a diagnosis of migraine with or without
aura as per the International Classification of Headache Disorders (ICHD)-3 criteria
and who have 14 or fewer headache days per month. The ICHD-3 defines chronic
migraine as patients with migraine that for at least 3 consecutive months
experience 15 or greater headache days a month, in which at least 8 of those
monthly headaches have the features of migraine.

Evidence and Uncertainties
• There are currently many approved therapies for the preventive treatment of
migraine, as well as many others that are used off-label.
• Recently, several therapies that act on the calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP)
pathway were approved for the preventive treatment of migraine (indicated for both
patients with episodic migraine and chronic migraine). These include the injectable
CGRP antagonist monoclonal antibodies, including erenumab, fremanezumab,
galcanezumab, and eptinezumab. Erenumab and galcanezumab are administered
as subcutaneous injections monthly; fremanezumab can be administered either
monthly or quarterly; eptinezumab is administered intravenously quarterly.
Rimegepant, a small molecule CGRP receptor antagonist, was also approved
recently for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine (this medication is also
indicated for the acute treatment of migraine).
• Other treatments approved for the preventive treatment of migraine include
onobotulinumtoxinA (approved only for the preventive treatment of chronic
migraine), and several oral drugs: propranolol, timolol, divalproex/sodium valproate,
and topiramate. Therapies for the preventive treatment of migraine that were
approved prior to 2010 have an indication that does not distinguish between
episodic or chronic migraine, as the classification into episodic or chronic migraine
only appeared a few years before. Most of the drugs currently marketed for the
preventive treatment of migraine demonstrate a reduction of monthly migraine days
of 0.7 to 2.0 days.
•

The efficacy of atogepant for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine was
demonstrated in two adequate and well-controlled trials, Study CGP-MD-01 and
Study 3101-301-002. Both studies used a well-validated and clinically meaningful
endpoint to establish efficacy, a reduction in the number of monthly migraine days
(MMD). Study CGP-MD-01 assessed five dosing regimens, daily doses of 10 mg,
30 mg, and 60 mg and twice daily doses of 30 mg and 60 mg, as compared to
placebo. All doses had statistically significantly greater reductions from baseline in
MMD compared to placebo: 1.2, 1.0, 0.7, 1.4, and 1.3 for atogepant groups of 10
mg daily (QD), 30 mg QD, 60 mg QD, 30 mg twice daily (BID), and 60 mg BID
groups, respecitvely Numerically, the twice-daily dosing regimens performed
slightly better than the daily dosing; however, only daily doses were carried forward
into the subsequent trial based on overall considerations, including the practicability
of twice-daily dosing in the setting of limited additional benefit. Study 3101-301-002
studied three dosing regimens, daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg, as
compared to placebo. All doses demonstrated efficacy on the primary endpoint with
a placebo corrected reduction from baseline in MMD of 1.2, 1.4, and 1.7 for the

Other drugs within the CGRP antagonist
class have been approved for the
preventive treatment of migraine. Most are

oral product,rimegepant, was initially
approved for the acute treatment of
migraine, and recently approved for the
preventive treatment of episodic migraine.
Atogepant will be the second oral CGRP
receptor antagonist marketed for the
preventive treatment of episodic migraine.
Rimegepant is dosed every other day
based on how the drug was studied in
clinical trials and has a postmarketing
commitment to study daily dosing, as such
a regimen would be consistent with the
drugs’ pharmacokinetic properities.

Atogepant at daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg
and 60 mg demonstrated efficacy for the
preventive treatment of episodic migraine
in adults.
Other marketed drugs for this indication
also have generally modest effect sizes,
typically reducing the number of monthly
migraine/headache days by 1 to 2 days;
like the other marketed products,
atogepant is not likely to render patients
migraine-free.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
Migraine is a condition that may result in significant morbidity and greatly impacts daily function. Although there are a number of
drugs approved for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in adults, additional treatment options remain important. Atogepant
is only the second oral CGRP antagonist to seek an indication for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in adults.
The Applicant has clearly demonstrated the efficacy of atogepant for the proposed indication in two 12-week placebo-controlled,
double-blind, parallel-arm clinical trials that enrolled adult migraine patients with 14 or fewer headache days per month. Daily
atogepant doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg, resulted in statistically significant reductions in the number of mean monthly migraine
days from baseline, relative to placebo (range 0.7 to 1.7 days). The treatment effect size is similar to that of other drugs approved for
8
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

the preventive treatment of migraine. Efficacy is further supported by significant treatment-related reductions, compared to placebo, in
the number of mean monthly headache days from baseline, reductions in acute medication use, increases in the number of patients
having 50% or greater reductions in their baseline migraine frequency, and by the results of patient-reported outcome measures
assessing the impact of migraine on daily functioning.
The safety database for atogepant was adequate for the proposed dosing regimens and the intended patient population. Overall,
atogepant has a favorable safety profile, and the safety findings can be adequately addressed in labeling and by routine and enhanced
pharmacovigilance. Common adverse reactions associated with treatment include constipation, nausea, fatigue, and decreased
appetite. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested for serious gastrointestinal toxicity, based on a signal for such events in the
CGRP antagonist class, along with a theoretical risk based on the presence of CGRP receptors in the gastrointestinal system. There
was no evidence to suggest the presence of an ischemic risk with atogepant, also a theoretical concern with CGRP antagonists;
however, enhanced pharmacovigilance for such events will be requested, consistent with the approach taken to all approved drugs of
this class. Although there was no signal of serious hepatotoxicity, increases in liver function tests associated with atogepant treatment
have been observed and will be described in labeling. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will also be requested for liver injury events.
Based upon review of all available efficacy and safety data, the benefits of atogepant clearly outweigh the risks for the preventive
treatment of episodic migraine in adults.

agents in this regard, hepatotoxicity remains a safety signal of interest in the small molecule
CGRP receptor antagonist development programs.
The review team identified six key review issues listed below; three that have a significant
impact on the overall determination of benefit of atogepant and three that contribute to the
understanding of safety to inform the benefit-risk profile and labeling.

3.1. Review Issue List
3.1.1. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Benefit
3.1.1.1. The Adequacy of Study CGP-MD-01 to Support an
Efficacy Claim
Assess if Study CGP-MD-01, a Phase 2/3 dose-finding trial, can serve as one of the two adequate
and well-controlled efficacy studies needed to support approval.
3.1.1.2. Should Atogepant 10 mg Daily Dose Be
Recommended for the General Population?
Assess if the 10 mg daily dose, in addition to the 30 mg and 60 mg daily doses, should be
recommended for the general population (and not just specific populations as the Applicant
proposes).
3.1.1.3. Are the AIM-D and MSQ v2.1 RFR Appropriate for
Inclusion in Labeling?
Evaluate if the Activity Impairment in Migraine Diary (AIM-D) Performance of Daily Activities
(PDA) domain score, the AIM-D Physical Impairment (PI) domain score, and the MigraineSpecific Quality of Life Questionnaire version 2.1 (MSQ v2.1) Role Function-Restrictive (RFR)
domain score results should be described in labeling.

3.1.2. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
3.1.2.1. Elevation of LFTs and CPK With Atogepant
Evaluate cases of elevated liver function tests (LFTs) and creatine phosphokinase (CPK),
specifically in the setting of a serious adverse event (SAE) in which elevated LFTs and CPK
were associated with a fatality.
3.1.2.2. Increased Ischemic Vascular Risk
Evaluate if there is an increased risk of vascular ischemia, specifically ischemic cardiovascular
events, hypertensive events, ischemic cerebrovascular events, or thrombotic events with the use
of atogepant.
3.1.2.3. Decreased Body Weight
Evaluate if decreased body weight is appropriate for inclusion in labeling.

3.2. Approach to the Review
Table 3 provides an overview of the clinical trials that were conducted to support an assessment
of the benefit-risk profile of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM in adults. The results
from the Phase 2/3 study, the Phase 3 study, and two long-term safety studies provide the
primary basis of evaluation of efficacy and safety of atogepant. Efficacy data supporting this
application will be discussed first, followed by an evaluation of the three Key Benefit Review
Issues (Section 6.3). The safety data supporting this application will then be discussed, followed
by an evaluation of the three Key Risk Review Issues (Section 7.7).

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical
Pharmacology
The clinical pharmacologic properties of atogepant were comprehensively evaluated as summarized in Table 5.
Table 5. Summary of General Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
Characteristic

Drug Information
Elimination
14
Following administration of 50 mg C-atogepant, 89% of the administered dose was recovered in the excreta; 81% of the
dose was recovered in feces (42% as unchanged) and 8% was recovered in urine (5% as unchanged). The major circulating
forms in plasma were atogepant (75%) and the major metabolite M23 (dioxygenated methylated glucuronide of atogepant,
15% of total exposure in plasma).
Based on the population PK analyses, the mean estimate of apparent clearance (CL/F) was 19 L/h.
Mean terminal half-life was approximately 11 hours.
Atogepant is metabolized primarily via CYP3A4.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Population PK analyses indicated that body weight has no clinically relevant impact on atogepant exposures.
Dose adjustment is not needed based on body weight.
Population PK analyses indicated that age has no clinically relevant impact on atogepant exposures.
Dose adjustment is not needed based on age.
No dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment.
Dose adjustment is needed in patient with severe renal impairment and ESRD patients (Section 8.1).
No dosage adjustment is required in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A or B).
In patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C), use of atogepant should be avoided (Section 8.1).
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Based on in vitro studies, atogepant is not an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or
CYP3A4, MAO-A, or UGT1A1. Atogepant does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 at clinically relevant
concentrations.
Atogepant does not inhibit P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, BSEP, MRP3, NTCP, MRP4, OCT1,
MATE1, or MATE2-K at clinically relevant concentrations.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Victim)
Atogepant is a substrate of CYP3A4. Dose adjustment is needed when co-administered with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors, and
strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers. No dose adjustment is needed when co-administered with moderate and weak
CYP3A4 inhibitors, and weak CYP3A4 inducers (Section 8.2).
Atogepant is a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3 and OAT1. Dose adjustment is needed when co-administered
with OATP inhibitors (Section 8.2).

Qulipta (atogepant)

5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
CGRP is thought to be involved in migraine based on studies reporting that administration of
CGRP can induce migraine-like headaches, that CGRP levels increase in the cranial circulation
during a migraine attack, and that CGRP can induce vasodilation (a proposed mechanism for the
underlying cause of migraine). In addition, CGRP antagonists have been shown to alleviate
migraine symptoms. Atogepant is a CGRP receptor antagonist.
In in vitro binding and functional assays, atogepant demonstrated low picomolar affinity and
antagonist activity against human and monkey CGRP receptors, and low nanomolar antagonist
activity against mouse, rat, rabbit, and dog CGRP receptors. In off-target binding assays (>120
receptors, enzymes, and ion channels), atogepant showed no affinity at up to micromolar
concentrations.
In isolated human coronary, meningeal, and cerebral artery preparations, atogepant (up to 1µM),
antagonized CGRP-induced relaxation. However, atogepant alone (up to 100 µM) did not exhibit
any vasoactivity. Atogepant inhibited capsaicin-induced dermal vasodilation at low nanomolar
concentrations in monkeys and low micromolar concentrations in rats and rabbits. In monkeys,
atogepant antagonized CGRP-induced increases in blood pressure and heart rate at low
nanomolar plasma concentrations.

6. Assessment of Effectiveness
6.1. Dose and Dose Responsiveness
The Applicant conducted one Phase 2/3 12-week dose-finding study, Study CGP-MD-01, to
determine the range of doses to move forward into a Phase 3 confirmatory efficacy study of
atogepant for the preventive treatment of migraine. Study CGP-MD-01 was designed to assess
both once daily (QD) (10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg) and twice daily (BID) dosing (30 mg and
60 mg [total daily doses of 60 mg and 120 mg, respectively]). The results of this study (discussed
in detail in Section 6.2.1.4) demonstrated preliminary efficacy of all doses with no clear evidence
of a dose-response relationship.
During the November 19, 2018, End of Phase 2 meeting, the Division stated that on face, Study
CGP-MD-01 had the characteristics of an adequate and well-controlled efficacy trial, but that a
final determination would be made during the review of the application. The doses to be studied
in the confirmatory efficacy trial (Study 3101-301-002) were discussed during a Type C meeting
in June 2017. At that time, the Division acknowledged that QD dosing may be more feasible for
patients than BID dosing, although BID dosing performed slightly better numerically with
respect to efficacy in Study CGP-MD-01. The Division recommended that the Applicant also
study the 10 mg QD dose in Study 3101-301-002, since efficacy of the 10 mg dose appeared
similar to that of the 30 mg and 60 mg QD dose.
A detailed discussion of the evaluation of any dose-response relationship based on the data from
these two efficacy studies is provided in Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.2 of this review.

Qulipta (atogepant)

6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate
Efficacy
6.2.1. Study CGP-MD-01
6.2.1.1. Design
Study CGP-MD-01 was a Phase 2/3 multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled,
parallel-group study that enrolled adult patients with a history of EM (4 to 14 migraine days per
month) from 78 sites in the United States. Patients were randomized to one of six different arms
in a 2:1:2:1:2:1 randomization sequence of placebo, atogepant 10 mg QD, atogepant 30 mg QD,
atogepant 30 mg BID, atogepant 60 mg QD, or atogepant 60 mg BID, respectively. The study
included a 4-week screening and baseline period, a 12-week double-blind treatment period, and a
4-week follow-up visit. The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean
monthly migraine days during the 12-week double-blind treatment period.
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria
Key inclusion criteria:
•

Male and female patients, ages 18 to 75, inclusive

•

1-year history of migraine with or without aura, consistent with the International
Classification of Headache Disorders

•

Age at onset of migraine prior to 50 years

•

History of 4 to 14 migraine/probable migraine days per month in prior 3 months and
baseline period

Key exclusion criteria:
•

History of chronic migraine, new persistent daily headache, trigeminal autonomic
cephalgia

•

≥15 headache days per month in prior 3 months or during baseline period

•

History of inadequate response to 3 or more medications used for preventive treatment of
migraine

•

Use of prohibited medications, use of opioids or barbiturates >2 days/month, triptans or
ergots ≥10 days/month or simple analgesics (aspirin, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory
drugs, acetaminophen) ≥15 days/month

•

Abnormal baseline laboratory or electrocardiogram (ECG) results

•

History of acute hepatitis or abnormal results on hepatitis screen, history of clinically
significant cardiovascular or cerebrovascular disease, baseline hypertension (>160 mm
mercury (Hg) for systolic blood pressure or diastolic blood pressure >100 mm Hg)

•

History of prior or current use of CGRP pathway drugs in the last 3 months

•

History of significant risk of self-harm based on responses on the Columbia-Suicide
Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)
20

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

Qulipta (atogepant)

•

Baseline alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) >1.5X the
upper limit normal (ULN), total bilirubin greater than >1X ULN, or serum albumin <2.8
gram/dL

•

Use of strong or moderate cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 inhibitors and inducers and
medications with demonstrated efficacy for the prevention of migraine (e.g., topiramate
and propranolol) in the 30 days prior to Visit 1 and throughout the study; botulinum toxin
injections were prohibited within the 6 months prior to Visit 1
6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan

Efficacy Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days
across the 12-week treatment period. Migraine days were defined as the combination of migraine
headache days and probable migraine headache days.
Secondary efficacy endpoints included (in hierarchical testing order):
•

Change from baseline in mean monthly headache days across the 12-week treatment
period.

•

Proportion of patients with at least a 50% reduction in mean monthly migraine days
across the 12-week treatment period.

•

Change from baseline in mean monthly acute medication use days across the 12-week
treatment period.

Efficacy Analysis Population
The efficacy analyses were based on the modified intent-to-treat population, which consisted of
all randomized participants who received at least one dose of study treatment, had an evaluable
baseline period of diary data, and had at least one evaluable postbaseline 4-week period (weeks
1 to 4, 5 to 8, and 9 to 12) of diary data. For baseline, evaluable is defined as having at least 20
days of diary data during the 4-week baseline period. For each postbaseline 4-week treatment
period, evaluable is defined as having at least 12 days of diary data for that particular period.
Analysis Methods
The primary endpoint was analyzed using mixed-effects model for repeated measures (MMRM).
The response variable was the change from baseline to each postbaseline month in monthly
migraine days. The model included baseline monthly migraine days as a covariate, treatment
group and visit (month) as fixed factors, and treatment group-by-visit and baseline-by-visit as
interaction terms. The restricted maximum likelihood method was used. The within-patient
correlation was modeled using the unstructured covariance matrix (compound symmetry
covariance matrix if convergence failed). The Kenward-Roger approximation was used to
estimate denominator degrees of freedom. Contrasts were constructed to obtain the average
treatment effects across the 12-week (Months 1 to 3) treatment period to compare each atogepant
treatment group versus the placebo group.

21
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Two sensitivity analyses were planned on the primary analysis. The first used a pattern-mixture
model approach based on the reference-based copy reference method for missing value
imputation. This analysis used a missing-not-at-random mechanism for missing data to assess the
impact of potential deviation from a missing at random assumption in the primary analysis. The
second sensitivity analysis used multiple imputations in conjunction with robust regression to
assess the robustness of the primary MMRM analysis with respect to the possibility of a
violation of the normality assumption. The normality assumption of MMRM analysis is usually
not a concern for trials of this size; therefore, the statistical review mainly focused on the
sensitivity analysis for missing data.
For secondary endpoints, pairwise comparisons were analyzed using MMRM for continuous
variables, and generalized linear mixed model for repeated measures of the binary response of at
least 50% reduction.
Multiplicity Adjustment
The overall type I error rate was controlled at α=0.05 for the set of primary and secondary
endpoint comparisons between each dose level of atogepant versus placebo using a graphical
approach with weighted-Bonferroni test procedure (Figure 1). The weighting strategy of the
multiple comparisons was designed to allocate initial alpha equally to the QD and BID dose
regimens. Within each dosing regimen, individual atogepant doses were tested in a hierarchical
order from high to low dose. In addition, for a given dose comparison versus placebo, the
primary endpoint and secondary endpoints were tested in a prespecified order. If the null
hypotheses for both the primary and secondary endpoints are rejected for one of the doses, the
associated alpha is passed to the other doses to increase the chances of success for the other
doses. For example, if the null hypotheses were rejected for all endpoints for the BID doses, the
60 mg QD dose would have 1/2x2/3=1/3 of the alpha from the testing of BID doses in addition
to the original half of the alpha. Hence the primary endpoint for 60 mg QD could be tested at a
significance level of 0.05*(1/2+1/3)=0.0417, and the adjusted p-value would be nominal
p-value*0.05/0.0417.

As indicated in the preceding tables, most enrolled patients were female, white, and had a mean
age of 39 to 42 years. Demographic data were generally balanced across groups. Disposition and
reasons for discontinuation did not indicate any clear trends, though the percentage of patients
who discontinued due to an adverse event (AE) was highest in the atogepant 60 mg BID study
arm by a small margin.
Primary Efficacy Endpoint
As indicated in Table 11, all 5 atogepant doses demonstrated statistically significant treatment
differences compared with placebo in the reduction of mean monthly migraine days during the
treatment period compared to baseline after multiplicity adjustment. The results of sensitivity
analyses (Section 16) were consistent with the results of the primary efficacy analysis.

Medical Officer’s Assessment: All doses demonstrated statistically significant reductions on the
primary endpoint and first secondary endpoint. The reduction in mean monthly migraine days of
0.7 to 1.15 days (difference from placebo) for the daily dosing regimens is in line with other
recently approved therapies that act on the CGRP pathway (range 0.7 to 2.0 reduction in mean
monthly migraine days). This degree of change was deemed to represent a clinically meaningful
change for the other approved therapies for the preventive treatment of migraine and this
reviewer concludes that the degree of change detected in this study also represents a clinically
meaningful change. No clear dose-response relationship was observed in this study. Refer to
details of this issue later in this review (Section 6.3.1).

6.2.2. Study 3101-301-002
6.2.2.1. Design
Study 3101-301-002 was a Phase 3 multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled,
parallel-group study which enrolled adult patients with a history of EM (4 to 14 migraine days
per month) from 128 sites in the United States. The primary efficacy endpoint was the change
from baseline in mean monthly migraine days during the 12-week double-blind treatment period.
Patients were randomized to one of four different arms in a 1:1:1:1 randomization sequence of
placebo, atogepant 10 mg QD, atogepant 30 mg QD, or atogepant 60 mg QD. Approximately
70% of randomized participants had to have taken at least one prior medication for the
preventive treatment of migraine, and randomization was stratified based on this prior exposure
(yes/no). The study included a 4-week screening and baseline period, a 12-week double-blind
treatment period, and a 4-week follow-up visit.
Medical Officer’s Assessment: A notable difference between this trial and Study CGP-MD-01
was the requirement that 70% of patients had to have taken at least one prior medication for the
preventive treatment of migraine. A discussion of the potential impact of this difference is
discussed in Section 6.3.1.
27
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria
Key inclusion criteria were the same as Study CGP-MD-01.
Key exclusion criteria were the same as Study CGP-MD-01 (Section 6.2.1.2 above), with the
following exceptions:
•

Patients with baseline ALT or AST >1X ULN, total bilirubin >1X ULN, serum albumin
<2.8 gram/dL, or estimated glomerular filtration rate <30 mL/min/1.73 m2 were also
excluded.

•

Patients on prior injectable monoclonal antibodies blocking the CGRP pathway within
the last 6 months and/or patients with a history of using more than three doses of
ubrogepant or rimegepant were excluded.

Medical Officer’s Assessment: The criteria for abnormal LFTs at baseline in this study are
stricter than the criteria listed for Study CGP-MD-01. Additionally, prior use of drugs or
biologics that act on the CGRP pathway required a longer time or more specific criteria since
last use compared to that of Study CGP-MD-01. Although these are relatively minor differences,
they may affect conclusions regarding hepatic safety or the general safety of atogepant when
used either as a single CGRP antagonist, or in combination with another product that acts on
the CGRP pathway.
6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan
Efficacy Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days
across the 12-week treatment period.
Secondary efficacy endpoints included (in hierarchical testing order):
•

Change from baseline in mean monthly headache days across the 12-week treatment
period

•

Change from baseline in mean monthly acute medication use days across the 12-week
treatment period

•

At least a 50% reduction in 3-month average of monthly migraine days

•

Change from baseline in MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score at week 12

•

Change from baseline in mean monthly PDA domain score of the AIM-D across the
12-week treatment period

•

Change from baseline in mean monthly PI domain score of the AIM-D across the
12-week treatment period

Efficacy Analysis Population
The efficacy analyses were conducted on the modified intent-to-treat population, which consisted
of all randomized participants who received at least one dose of study intervention, had an
evaluable baseline period of eDiary data, and at least one evaluable postbaseline 4-week period
(Weeks 1 to 4, 5 to 8, and 9 to 12) of eDiary data during the double-blind treatment period. For
baseline, evaluable is defined as having at least 20 days of diary data during the 4-week baseline
28
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

period. For each postbaseline 4-week treatment period, evaluable is defined as having at least 14
days of diary data for that particular period.
Analysis Methods
The primary endpoint was analyzed using MMRM. The statistical model included treatment
group, visit, prior exposure (yes/no) to a migraine preventive medication, and treatment groupby-visit interaction as categorical fixed effects. It also included the baseline score and baselineby-visit interaction as covariates. An unstructured covariance matrix was used to model the
covariance of within-participant repeated measurements. The Kenward-Roger approximation
was used to estimate the denominator degrees of freedom.
Sensitivity analyses for missing data handling were planned, including: (1) analysis of
covariance model based on 3-month average of the monthly migraine days, (2) MMRM based on
primary measures collected during the double-blind and follow-up periods, (3) analysis of
covariance with multiple imputations within group, and (4) a pattern-mixture model approach
based on the reference-based copy reference method. Sensitivity analysis using multiple
imputations in conjunction with robust regression were conducted to assess the robustness of the
primary MMRM analysis to the possible violation of the normality assumption.
The 50% or greater responder endpoint, where responders were defined as a participant with at
least 50% reduction in 3-month average of monthly migraine days, was analyzed using a logistic
regression model. The other secondary efficacy endpoints were analyzed in the same manner as
that was used to analyze the primary endpoint.
Multiplicity Adjustment
The overall type I error rate was controlled at α=0.05 for the set of primary and secondary
endpoint comparisons between each dose level of atogepant versus placebo using a graphical
approach with weighted-Bonferroni test procedure (Figure 3). The initial allocation of the overall
significance level to three primary hypotheses was 1/3 of the overall significance level for each
dose. Within each individual dose, testing started from the primary endpoint, and then the
secondary endpoints in a prespecified order. If the null hypotheses for both the primary and the
first three secondary endpoints were rejected for one of the doses, 1/3 of the associated alpha was
passed to the other doses (1/6 fraction for each dose) to increase the chances of success for the
other doses in testing endpoints in the primary positions of the hierarchy, and the remaining 2/3
of the associated alpha was reserved for testing null hypothesis endpoints within the same dose.
If hypotheses for three null hypothesis endpoints were rejected within a dose based on the
remaining alpha, the alpha for this dose was to be propagated to the other two doses to make full
use of the alpha.

Medical Officer’s Assessment: Study 3101-301-002 demonstrated statistically significant
treatment effects favoring atogepant on all primary and secondary endpoints for all doses, with
the exception of the 10 mg dose of atogepant on the clinical outcome assessments, the AIM-D
PDA domain score and the AIM-D PI domain score (although there was a nonsignificant
numerical benefit at this dose). The effect sizes for the primary efficacy endpoint of the change
from baseline in mean monthly migraine days were generally consistent with those observed in
Study CGP-MD-01; the clinical meaningfulness of which has been discussed previously in this
review.
Unlike Study CGP-MD-01, there was a slight numerical dose-response relationship for all
efficacy endpoints, with higher doses demonstrating greater benefit. The 1:1:1:1 allocation of
patients to treatments in Study 3101-301-002 may have allowed for an increased ability to
identify the apparent dose-response trend. Another factor that may have led to the more
predictable estimates for the treatment effect, and thus to seeing a numerical dose response for
the doses tested, was the requirement for this study that 70% of patients had to have taken a
prior preventive medication, which may have led to a slight reduction in the placebo response
rate in this study (-2.48) compared to Study CGP-MD-01 (-2.85). It is important to note that this
study was not enriched for prior treatment failures in a manner that could support any related
labeling claims. As per the recommendations for study designs described in the Guidance for
Industry: Enrichment Strategies for Clinical Trials to Support Approval of Human Drugs and
Biological Products, 1 a trial which uses an enrichment strategy of including patients who were
treatment failures, nonresponders to a different drug should be randomized to the new drug or to
the drug they did not respond to. This Applicant did not employ this strategy. Historical account
1

We update guidances periodically. For the most recent version of a guidance, check the FDA guidance web page at
https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents.

34
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

of taking a prior drug for preventive treatment of migraine does not necessarily mean
nonresponse or response to that drug.

6.3. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Benefit
6.3.1. The Adequacy of Study CGP-MD-01 to Support
an Efficacy Claim
Issue
Assess if Study CGP-MD-01, a Phase 2/3 dose-finding trial, can serve as one of the two adequate
and well-controlled efficacy studies needed to support approval.
Background
A number of drugs have been approved for the preventive treatment of migraine in adults in
recent years, including five drugs that act on the CGRP pathway. Following review of Study
CGP-MD-01, the Applicant concluded that, although initially designed as a Phase 2/3 dosefinding study, this trial could be considered an adequate and well-controlled study that could be
submitted in the current application as one of the two pivotal efficacy studies required to support
the approval of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM. This was discussed with the
Division at the November 19, 2018 End of Phase 2 meeting, and the Division concurred that on
face, this study appeared to have the characteristics of such a study, but that the final
determination would be a matter of review.
During the new drug application (NDA) review, the Division evaluated the following specific
issues with respect to the design and strength of the findings from Study CGP-MD-01:
•

The trial was originally designed as a Phase 2/3 dose-finding study and not as a study
intended to support marketing approval, so the clinical review team evaluated whether
the trial had the characteristics needed to consider this an adequate and well-controlled
trial.

•

Though the trial demonstrated a statistically significant effect on the prespecified primary
endpoint, the treatment effect size (placebo corrected reduction of 0.7 to 1.1 monthly
migraine days) was on the lower end of what was seen with other products marketed for
this indication (placebo corrected reduction of 0.7 to 2.0 monthly migraine days).

•

There was an inverse numerical dose-response relationship, with the highest numerical
treatment effect in the QD dose arms occurring in the lowest dose of 10 mg QD and
progressively lower numerical effects in the 30 mg and 60 mg QD dose arms. The BID
dosing arms had a greater numerical benefit than the QD dosing arms, but, again, the
60 mg QD arm had a smaller numerical effect size as compared to the 30 mg BID arm.

•

Although all numerically favoring atogepant, the secondary endpoints (beyond the first
secondary endpoint of change from baseline in mean monthly headache days) did not
reach statistical significance in any arm of patients treated with daily atogepant compared
to placebo. (Section 6.2.1.4)
35

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Assessment
In order to determine whether Study CGP-MD-01 could serve as one of the two adequate and
well-controlled efficacy trials to support the current application, this reviewer conducted an
assessment to evaluate why the findings in this study appeared to be less robust to those of Study
3101-301-002. This assessment is described below.
The study design and many characteristics of Study CGP-MD-01 were identical to that of Study
3101-301-002, except that in Study 3101-301-002, at least 70% of patients were required to have
taken at least one prior preventive treatment for migraine. Study 3101-301-002 was stratified for
this factor and Study CGP-MD-01 was not. In Study CGP-MD-01, only 30% percent of patients
had tried a prior preventive treatment for migraine. As stated previously, it is possible that the
enrollment of a greater percentage of patients who were naïve to preventive treatments for
migraine in Study CGP-MD-01 accounts for the somewhat higher placebo response in this study,
thereby decreasing the ability to demonstrate statistical significance on the analyses of several
secondary efficacy measures.
The Applicant conducted a post hoc analysis of Study CGP-MD-01 comparing the reduction
from baseline in the number of monthly migraines over the 12-week treatment period in the
subgroup of patients who had taken prior preventive treatment to those who had not (treatmentnaïve). This analysis demonstrated that in patients who had previously tried a prior therapy for
the preventive treatment of migraine, the effect size was greater (-1.38, -1.51, -2.17 for 10 mg,
30 mg, and 60 mg atogepant, respectively) between atogepant and placebo arms compared to
treatment-naïve patients (-0.81, -1.05, -0.66 for 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg atogepant,
respectively). The Applicant concluded that the slightly higher placebo response rate seen in
Study CGP-MD-01 (-2.85 days) as compared to that of Study 3101-301-002 (-2.48 days) may
have contributed to this difference. Please see Section 16 (sensitivity analyses) for results of
these analyses. In addition, Study CGP-MD-01 utilized a 2:1:2:1:2:1 randomization ratio unlike
the 1:1:1:1 randomization ratio used in Study 3101-301-002, and this unequal allocation may
have biased patients leading to a heightened expectation of therapeutic benefit in Study CGPMD-01 (Hey and Kimmelman 2014).
Conclusion
The review team, including the medical officer, clinical pharmacology, and statistical reviewers,
has assessed the design, results, and analysis of Study CGP-MD-01 to evaluate whether this
study can be considered an adequate and well-conducted trial to support approval of this
application. The team determined that although there are some limitations identified in the trial
results, the data provided still support efficacy of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM in
adults. Overall, despite the presence of an inverse numerical dose response, the primary endpoint
was positive for all doses, and the secondary endpoints that did not reach statistical significance
demonstrated a trend in the correct direction. There was a numerically higher treatment effect in
the BID dosing groups as compared to the QD dosing groups, again suggesting the presence of a
higher treatment effect and biological activity at these higher exposures. In addition, the unequal
allocation of treatment arms in this study and the higher percentage of treatment naive patients,
may have increased the placebo response rate, making it more difficult to detect a treatment
effect. Ultimately, the data were consistent with atogepant’s efficacy. Therefore, the team
concludes that this study can serve as an adequate and well-controlled trial in support of the
efficacy of atogepant.
36
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

6.3.2. Should the Atogepant 10 mg Daily Dose Be
Recommended for the General Population?
Issue
Assess if the 10 mg daily dose, in addition to the 30 mg and 60 mg daily doses, should be
recommended for the general population (and not just specific populations as the Applicant
proposes).
Background
The Applicant studied several doses of atogepant, including daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, 60 mg
and twice daily doses of 30 mg and 60 mg, in Study CGP-MD-01. The Applicant only studied
the three daily doses in Study 3101-301-002. The Applicant initially proposed the 10 mg QD
dose be marketed only for specific populations, namely patients with kidney disease (see
Pharmacology discussion, Section 8.1 for their final recommendation) and patients taking
concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitors; however, it may be advantageous to include the option of a
lower effective dose to the general population.
Assessment
Atogepant was effective at all doses studied in Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002 (Section
6.2, Table 11, Table 12, Table 16, and Table 17) The primary efficacy endpoint, the change from
baseline in mean monthly migraine days over the double-blind treatment period, demonstrated
positive results in both trials. In Study CGP-MD-01, the 10 mg daily dose had the numerically
largest treatment effect compared to other groups, with an inverse numerical dose-response
relationship observed when considering all daily doses studied, although the twice daily dosing
regimens did perform numerically better than the daily dosing regimens. In Study 3101-301-002,
there was a slight numerical dose- response relationship for the three daily doses studied (10 mg,
30 mg, and 60 mg).
Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002 demonstrated statistically significant results for all doses
on the key secondary endpoint, change from baseline in mean monthly headache days. Study
3101-301-002 also demonstrated statistically significant results for all doses on secondary
endpoints of change from baseline in acute medication use, ≥50% reduction in mean monthly
migraine days, and change from baseline in the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score. The 30 mg and
60 mg dose arm also demonstrated statistically significant results on the analysis of the change
from baseline for the AIM-D PDA domain score and the AIM-D PI domain score, while the
10 mg arm did not (see Section 6.3.1).
Given the consistency of results at the 10 mg daily dose with that of the 30 mg and 60 mg daily
doses, and the generally flat-dose response relationship across the range of doses investigated,
there is support for approving the 10 mg dose in addition to the other doses proposed by the
Applicant (30 mg and 60 mg). As discussed in Section 7.6 of this review, the safety profile for
atogepant demonstrates a dose-related increase for some of the adverse reactions, such as nausea,
decreased appetite, somnolence, and decreased weight.

37
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Conclusion
The 10 mg daily dose demonstrated consistent efficacy across a range of clinically meaningful
endpoints in Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002. The lack of a statistically significant
treatment effect of this dose on the AIM-D scales in Study 3101-301-002, especially in the
setting of a numerical benefit, should not preclude the approval of this dose for the general
population.
In considering whether to approve this lower dose, the safety profile of atogepant should also be
considered. As discussed in detail in the safety sections below, there did appear to be a doserelated increase in several commonly reported adverse reactions, including nausea, decreased
appetite, fatigue, and decreased weight. This supports the approval of the lowest effective dose.
The recommended doses of atogepant are 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg QD for the general
population.

6.3.3. Are the AIM-D and MSQ v2.1 RFR Appropriate
for Inclusion in Labeling?
Issue
Evaluate if the results of the analyses of the AIM-D PDA domain score, AIM-D PI domain
score, and MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score should be described in labeling.
Background
The Applicant proposes including in labeling the results of the analyses of three clinical outcome
assessment (COA) measures that were included as hierarchically-controlled secondary endpoints
in Study 3101-301-002. To support these claims, the Applicant submitted a COA evidence
dossier for the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain and the AIM-D.
Assessment
The following COAs were prespecified as hierarchically-controlled secondary endpoints in the
Study 3101-301-002.
•

MSQ v2.1 RFR domain:
— Evaluates the frequency of the impact of migraine on daily social and work activities
— Has a 0 to 100 scale, with higher scores indicating better daily functioning (i.e., lesser
impact of migraine)

•

The AIM-D PDA domain
— Assesses the difficulty in performance of daily activities due to migraine
— Has a 0 to 100 scale, with higher scores indicating worse performance of daily
activities

•

AIM-D PI domain:
— Assesses physical impairment due to migraine

Has a 0 to 100 scale, with higher scores indicating worse physical impairment. The conceptual

Anchor-based analyses were conducted using data from Study 3101-301-002 to derive thresholds

improvement thresholds are consistent with the thresholds presented in the literature, which
relied on receiver operating characteristic methods to incorporate various anchors into the
psychometric analysis (Speck et al. 2019). However, a one-point improvement as specified a
priori by the Applicant is not always sufficient, and the interpretation of within patient change
employing a two-point improvement was also considered in our interpretation of clinically
meaningful improvement on the clinical efficacy endpoints.

Conclusion
The MSQ v2.1 RFR domain, AIM-D PDA domain, and AIM-D PI domain measures appear
fit-for-purpose in this context of use, and the corresponding clinical efficacy endpoints are
defined appropriately and positioned as alpha-controlled endpoints in the endpoint model.
Therefore, these measures can be described in labeling. For future drug development, we
recommend that Applicants focus on measuring the most relevant and important concepts to
patients rather than a broad, disease-specific, health-related quality of life measurement. With
50
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

that respect, the AIM-D domains are more consistent, interpretable, and sensitive to the apparent
dose-response relationship reflected in the primary and other key secondary endpoints (see Table
17).
The results of the COA measures based on the above three clinical efficacy endpoints have been
confirmed by the FDA Statistical Reviewer. The anchor-based analysis results of the COA
endpoints showed the following:
•

There is a clear separation between all three treatment arms and placebo arm in MSQ
v2.1 RFR domain across the range of clinically meaningful thresholds, suggesting that all
three treatment arms had clinically meaningful within-patient improvements in the impact
of migraine on daily social and work activities, as assessed by the MSQ v2.1 RFR
domain score.

•

There is a more complex pattern of results in the anchor-based analysis of the AIM-D
endpoints.
— There is a modest separation between the 30 mg and 60 mg treatment arms and the
placebo arm in both AIM-D PDA and PI domains across the range of clinically
meaningful thresholds, suggesting that both 30 mg and 60 mg treatment arms had
clinically meaningful within-patient improvement in performance of daily activities
due to migraine and reduction in physical impairment due to migraine.
— There is a small separation between the 10 mg treatment arm and the placebo arm in
both AIM-D PAD and PI domains within the range of clinically meaningful
thresholds, suggesting that the 10 mg treatment arm improves performance of daily
activities due to migraine, as assessed by the AIM-D PDA, and reduces physical
impairment due to migraine, as assessed by the PI domain scores, but minimally.
— Overall, there is a clear dose-response relationship observable in the change from
baseline results for the AIM-D domains, which is consistent with other clinical
efficacy endpoints (e.g., headache days and acute medication use).

•

Given the conceptual overlap between the MSQ v2.1 and the AIM-D, consideration
should be given in the label to avoid overweighting the apparent treatment benefit. For
example, listing overlapping items twice in the label to document the two COA measures
or presenting multiple p-values that document a common inference may be misleading.

7. Risk and Risk Management
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
A complete nonclinical package was submitted to the NDA, which included general toxicology
studies of up to 6 and 9 months’ duration in rat and monkey, respectively; a standard battery of
genetic toxicology assays (Ames, in vitro clastogenicity in CHO cells, and in vivo rat
micronucleus); 2-year carcinogenicity studies in mouse and rat; and reproductive and
development studies (fertility and early embryonic, embryofetal development, and pre- and
postnatal development studies in rat; embryofetal development study in rabbit).
51
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Dosing in the pivotal general toxicology studies was based on decreases in body weight gain in
rats and emesis in monkeys in the nonpivotal studies. No target organ toxicity was identified in
the general toxicology studies.
Atogepant was negative in Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development-compliant
Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and rat bone marrow micronucleus assays. Atogepant
was not carcinogenic in 2-year mouse and rat studies.
The following safety concerns were identified:
(1) In the 2-year carcinogenicity study conducted in male and female mice, females were not
adequately evaluated because none of the criteria for an adequate high dose (HD) was
met.
(2) In animal studies, oral administration of atogepant during organogenesis resulted in
embryolethality, adverse effects on embryofetal development (decreased fetal body
weight in rats, increased incidences of fetal variations in rats and rabbits) at maternal
exposures greater those used clinically.
The nonclinical data support approval of atogepant. However, to address the concerns regarding
the assessment of carcinogenic potential, the sponsor will need to conduct a dose range-finding
study to determine if the HD in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in female mice was adequate
(e.g., a maximum tolerated dose or maximum feasible dose). If the HD is determined not to be
adequate, the carcinogenicity study will need to be repeated in female mice with an adequate
HD. These may be conducted as postmarketing requirements.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
The following potential risks based on drug class or other drug-specific factors have been
identified for evaluation in this review:
•

Several oral CGRP receptor antagonists that are no longer in development caused serious
cases of drug-induced liver injury (DILI). Modifications were made to drugs developed
in this class, resulting in the development of products, such as atogepant, that are not
expected to be associated with this safety finding (such findings have not been associated
with either ubrogepant or rimegepant, to date). However, given the history of DILI in
previously developed oral CGRP antagonists, liver injury was analyzed as a safety signal
of interest in this review (see Section 7.7.1).

•

Since CGRP is thought to play a role in compensatory vasodilatation in the setting of
ischemia, there is a theoretical concern that CGRP inhibition may increase the risk of
cardiovascular and cerebrovascular events. Although an increased risk of such events has
not been identified as a safety signal in the other marketed products in this class, this risk
is evaluated as a safety signal of interest in this review (see Section 7.7.2).

•

Due to the diffuse presence of CGRP receptors in the gastrointestinal system, and the
labeled Warning for constipation with erenumab, the effects on this system, including the
risk of constipation, were assessed as a safety area of interest in this review (see Section
7.7.3).
52

7.3. Potential Safety Concerns Identified Through
Postmarket Experience
Not applicable.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The safety review focuses on the safety of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM in
adults. The data sources include the results from Studies CGP-MD-01, 3101-301-002, 3101-302002, and 3101-309-002 (refer to Table 3). The safety population is defined as any patient who
received one or more doses of atogepant in any of these studies. The cut-off date for the original
submission was May 25, 2020. The Applicant provides safety data in the Integrated Summary of
Safety (ISS) based on the following groups:
•

Group 1: Controlled clinical efficacy studies (CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002) involving
several doses of atogepant (10 mg, 30 mg, 60 mg, 30 mg BID, and 60 mg BID) or
placebo (Section 6.2)

•

Group 2: Open-label long-term safety studies (3101-302-002 and 3101-309-002) with
atogepant 60 mg or standard of care (SoC) (Section 7.6)

•

Group 3: Group 1 and Group 2 pooled

In this review, summaries of the Applicant’s findings with confirmatory analyses conducted by
the Clinical Data Scientist, Dr. Anne Bunner, will be presented. AE tables in this review refer to
treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) by “actual dose” treatment group (the dose the
patient received). Tables are either reproduced from the Applicant’s submission (where noted) or
created by Dr. Bunner.
All placebo-controlled data will be included in tables and figures, and data from the long-term
safety studies will be included, only when indicated. All doses (including the 30 mg BID and
60 mg BID doses) will be examined to provide the most complete information regarding
evidence of dose-related AEs. When discussing ISS Group 2, only the 60 mg dose was
evaluated, since this is the highest dose proposed for marketing and the only dose studied in the
long-term safety studies.
A 120-Day Safety Update Report was submitted on May 19, 2021 (which included a cut-off date
of November 13, 2020). The report includes an additional 30 patients who were exposed to
atogepant 60 mg daily and additional long-term exposure data for patients enrolled in extension
Study 3101-309-002.
The 120-Day Safety Update also included safety information from four additional ongoing
studies regarding patients with elevated LFTs (per the Division’s request for an analysis of
postbaseline aminotransferase elevations in ongoing studies at the September 22, 2020 preNDA
meeting). The ongoing studies include Study 3101-303-002 (a double-blind, randomized,
placebo-controlled trial of atogepant for the preventive treatment of CM), Study 3101-306-002
(the long-term extension study for 3101-303-002), and two clinical pharmacology studies. These
studies are not reviewed here; however, the pertinent hepatic safety data from these studies is
presented as part of Section 7.7.1.

safety study (i.e., had not previously received atogepant). Importantly, there could be a gap of 6
months up to 2 years from the time of the completion of the placebo-controlled study to
enrollment in the long-term safety study (Study 3101-302-002), due to a delay in its initiation.

At the time of the cutoff date for the original application, Studies CGP-MD-01, 3101-301-002
and 3101-302-002 were complete. Only Study 3101-309-002 was still ongoing, and the
Applicant submitted a 120-Day Safety Update on May 19, 2021, to provide updated safety
analyses. Per this safety update, as of November 13, 2020, 2,479 unique participants were
exposed to at least one dose of atogepant during the clinical development program; 997 of these
participants received atogepant for ≥6 months (715 at the 60 mg once-daily dose) and 670
received atogepant for ≥12 months (469 at the 60 mg once-daily dose). The updated overall
mean treatment duration in the long-term safety studies was 278.9 days in the standard of care
group and 253.9 days in the atogepant 60 mg once daily group.

Table 26 shows that more patients discontinued drug in the SoC arm than the atogepant arm in

7.6.2. Deaths
There were no deaths in the placebo-controlled studies, and two deaths in the open-label longterm safety studies. One death in Study 3101-302-002 was in a 50-year-old female who was the
victim of homicide, and this case is not summarized in this review as it was assessed as not
related to atogepant by FDA reviewers. The second death (due to beta hemolytic streptococcal
infection) is reviewed in detail in Section 7.7.1 and was also assessed as not related to atogepant
use by FDA reviewers.

7.6.3. Serious Adverse Events
There were eight SAEs in the placebo-controlled safety population in the atogepant arms and
five SAEs in the placebo arms. These are reviewed below.
There were no trends in SAEs by organ class or by preferred terms.

(b) (6)

(3) Patient
was a 55-year-old white male with a history of migraine,
gastrointestinal reflux disease, seasonal allergy, and chronic urticaria who was
randomized to atogepant 10 mg QD who experienced acute right upper quadrant
abdominal pain and cholelithiasis of severe intensity on Day 34 that resolved the same

right upper quadrant abdominal pain. Work-up revealed a distended gallbladder, with
cholelithiasis, potential acute cholecystitis, and no gross stone in the common bile duct.
The patient was treated in the emergency room with resolution of the pain. The next
morning (Day 39) the patient had recurrent pain and was admitted with a diagnosis of
cholecystitis (SAE). The patient underwent a cholecystectomy successfully and was
discharged on Day 40. The patient completed study treatment with no further events;
therefore, a relationship of this event to atogepant use is unlikely.

(4) Patient
was a 28-year-old white female with a history of migraine who

7.6.10.4 Suicidality.

ruled out in the case of the 41-year-old female (Patient
described above, the first
three SAEs listed do not appear to be related to study drug. The overdose SAE is discussed
separately in Section 7.6.10.4 Suicidality.
Study 3101-301-002
There were four total treatment-emergent SAEs (two in the 10 mg arm [described below] and
two in the placebo arm).
•

(b) (6)

Patient
was a 23-year-old female randomized to atogepant 10 mg who
experienced an event of optic neuritis on Day 23 of the study. Her ophthalmological
exam was inconsistent and variable, MRI brain and orbits, and visual-evoked potentials
were reported as normal. She was treated with steroids and improved on Day 108.
Atogepant was discontinued after diagnosis. The patient has a positive family history of
optic neuritis and multiple sclerosis. The Applicant assessed the event as not likely to
represent optic neuritis and not related to study intervention.

Medical Officer’s Assessment: This reviewer disagrees with the Applicant and believes this
event could represent optic neuritis. Additionally, a role of atogepant in this event cannot be
entirely ruled out since the event did occur close to initiation of this drug; however, a
relationship to atogepant remains unlikely given the patient’s age, family history, and the
increased risk of clinically isolated events in her demographic group. The patient’s risk factors
confound assessing the relationship between atogepant initiation and the optic neuritis event. In
addition, there were no other similar cases observed in the development program, even though
patients in this database are in the demographic group that would have a high risk for
developing multiple sclerosis or optic neuritis.
•

(b) (6)

Patient
was a 56-year-old black female with an extensive past medical history
(including asthma, tobacco use, obesity, hypertension, drug abuse, and sleep apnea) who
experienced an SAE of an asthma attack on Day 1 of receiving study medication
(atogepant 10 mg daily). The day of treatment (Day 1), she started feeling an
“itchy/scratchy” throat with shortness of breath and wheezing. Atogepant was
59

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

temporarily interrupted on Day 2. On Day 4, she went to the emergency room and was
treated for an asthma attack. She was started on steroids and nebulizer treatments. She
also experienced myalgia of moderate severity and musculoskeletal pain with coughing.
On Day 6, she reported nausea and was discharged from the hospital. Atogepant was
resumed on Day 9. On Day 11, she experienced pelvic pain and hematuria. These events
were not considered related to treatment. She was withdrawn from the study due to the
pelvic pain and hematuria and did not complete the safety follow-up visit.
Medical Officer’s Assessment: The Applicant assessed these events as not related to treatment.
It is difficult to assess causality due to multiple confounders; however, it is possible that
atogepant use led to the initial asthma attack, although the mechanism for such an association is
unclear. The patient was on concomitant medications for asthma and was an active smoker. The
events of myalgia and musculoskeletal pain may be associated with atogepant (this is reviewed
separately as part of Section 7.7.1), but these events are also confounded in this patient due to
multiple factors.
Group 2 SAEs
In the long-term safety study, Study 3101-302-002, there were 24 SAEs in the atogepant 60 mg
once daily arm and 7 SAEs in the SoC arm. There were no trends in SAEs by organ class or by
preferred terms, and individual SAEs pertaining to specific organ systems are evaluated
separately (e.g., suicidality, beta hemolytic streptococcal infection).
The following is a notable case of a generalized seizure that occurred in the long-term safety
study.
•

(b) (6)

Patient
was a 31-year-old female receiving atogepant 60 mg in the long-term
safety study who discontinued due to a seizure on Day 33 of treatment with atogepant.
This patient did not report that she had a history of epilepsy, for which she was taking
topiramate, at screening. The patient had a history of complex partial seizures, was status(b) (6)
post-amygdalohippocampectomy in
, and experienced an SAE of generalized seizure
(after a 10-year history of not having generalized seizures and only having continued
complex partial seizures). She was discontinued from the study. It is unlikely the seizure
was related to atogepant, given the patient’s epilepsy diagnosis and the much greater
likelihood of the occurrence representing a breakthrough seizure.

7.6.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to
Adverse Events
In Study CGP-MD-01, three patients discontinued due to an SAE (two patients in an atogepant
arm and one patient in the placebo arm). Five patients discontinued due to elevated LFTs: three
patients discontinued due to elevated ALT or AST, one in the atogepant 10 mg QD arm and two
in the 60 mg atogepant QD arm. These patients are further described in Section 7.7.1.
In Study 3101-301-002, three patients discontinued due to an SAE (two in the atogepant 10 mg
arm and one in the placebo arm). There were no discontinuations due to cardiovascular or LFT
abnormalities. One patient in the placebo arm discontinued because of suicidal ideation.

In the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1), 50 (3.9%) patients in the atogepant arms and 11
(2.7%) patients in the placebo arms discontinued due to a TEAE. Common AEs leading to
discontinuation in the atogepant arms included constipation (7 patients), nausea (6 patients),
fatigue (5 patients, with 3 patients in 60 mg BID arm), and dizziness (4 patients).
Though constipation and nausea were the most common reasons for discontinuation in the
placebo-controlled trials, the absolute number of patients who discontinued due to these TEAEs
was small. There were no dose-related trends for discontinuation. TEAEs that led to
discontinuation in less than 2 atogepant-treated patients are not included to simplify the table
above, and because these are less likely to be related to atogepant in the absence of any trends.
The full table is included in Section 17 (Table 127).
In the long-term safety studies (ISS Group 2), the most common reasons for discontinuation in
the atogepant-treated arms were nausea or vomiting in seven patients (0.6%), fatigue or
somnolence in five patients (0.4%), and abdominal pain in four patients (0.3%). Please see Table
128 in Section 17 for the full table in ISS Group 2.

Group 3 – Pooled Controlled and Long-Term Safety Studies
TEAEs occurring in Group 3 are displayed in Table 33. Group 3 includes patients with the
longest exposure to atogepant (up to 15 months of treatment in some patients). Constipation had
the highest risk difference in atogepant-treated patients as compared to the SoC arm. Nausea and
decreased appetite occurred slightly more frequently in the atogepant treated patients than those
in the SoC arm.

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

An increase in sodium to 145 to 149 mEq/L was observed in 10.8% of atogepant-treated patients
compared to 7.8% of placebo-treated patients at any time in the study. Sodium levels did not
trend higher than 155 for any patient, and only three patients (0.23%) in the atogepant arms had a
level 2 sodium increase (>150). There were no reported AEs associated with these laboratory
abnormalities. These findings likely reflect natural variations over time as there is no clear
mechanism for this finding based on what is currently known about the pharmacodynamics of
atogepant.
An increase in potassium of 5.6 to 5.9 mEq/L at any time was observed in 70 (5.4%) patients in
the atogepant arms compared to 15 (3.8%) patients in the placebo arm, with a risk difference of
1.64 (-0.6 to 3.87). There were no reported AE terms associated with these findings. These
findings likely reflect natural variation over time as there is no clear mechanism for this finding
either, based on what is currently known about pharmacodynamics of atogepant.
Lastly, the bicarbonate levels in 227 (17.5%) of atogepant-treated patients were lower than
20 mEq/L at some point during the study compared to 63 (15.8%) of patients in the placebo arm.
The risk difference is 1.71 (-2.42 to 5.84). This may be due to some degree of hyperventilation or
metabolic acidosis, but the mechanism of this is unclear, and the changes are similar in the
placebo arm (with a smaller population of patients) with a less than 2% risk difference.
Kidney function
Laboratory values related to kidney function were evaluated over the course of the placebocontrolled trials (ISS Group 1). There were no patients in either atogepant or placebo groups with
elevated creatinine of at least 1.5X baseline values during the study.

There were similar fluctuations in CPK values across all treatment arms, including placebo, with

7.6.8. QT Interval Evaluation
This application was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for cardiac safety studies.
The Interdisciplinary Review Team concludes that there was no significant corrected QT interval
prolongtation effect of atogepant detected.
The Applicant conducted a thorough study (CGP-PK-04) and evaluated the highest dose of
300 mg atogepant. The results of an analysis of the data from this study did not suggest that
atogepant is associated with a significant corrected QT interval prolonging effect (Table 41).
Nonclinical data support this finding.

7.6.9. 120-Day Safety Update
As of November 13, 2020, 2,479 unique participants were exposed to at least one dose of
atogepant during the clinical development program for episodic migraine; 997 of these
participants received atogepant for ≥6 months (715 at 60 mg QD) and 670 received atogepant for
≥12 months (469 at 60 mg QD).
There were no deaths since the May 25, 2020 cut-off date for the initial NDA.
