NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

There were six additional treatment-emergent SAEs (gastritis, Clostridium difficile colitis, road
traffic accident and concussion, basal cell carcinoma, prostate cancer, and ureterolithiasis), all of
which are considered unrelated to study drug.
There were no reports of suicidal ideation with intent to act or of suicidal behavior in Study
3101-309-002.
One additional participant in Study 3101-309-002 (atogepant 60 mg once daily) had a treatmentemergent AST value ≥3X ULN since the safety reporting cut-off for the initial NDA; the Clinical
Adjudication Committee adjudicated the elevation as unlikely related to atogepant.
As of January 31, 2021, a total of five participants had ALT and/or AST value ≥3X ULN
reported during participation in ongoing studies (one study in CM and one pharmacokinetics
[PK] study). Please see review in Section 7.7.1 by Dr. Paul Hayashi for details of these cases and
assessment.

7.6.10. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
7.6.10.1. Cardiovascular (Including Hypertension),
Cerebrovascular, or Peripheral Vascular Disease
Please refer to Section 7.7.2 for a detailed description of this safety issue and an analysis of
findings in this application.
7.6.10.2. Hepatotoxicity
Please refer to Section 7.7.1 for a detailed description of this safety issue and an analysis of
findings in this application.
7.6.10.3. Gastrointestinal Disorders
Gastrointestinal disorders have been associated with CGRP antagonism, and the erenumab label
has a Warning for constipation, including the potential for serious cases. A review of
gastrointestinal AEs was conducted to assess if there is an increased risk of these events
associated with atogepant use.
Evaluation of TEAEs demonstrated that constipation and nausea were among the most
commonly reported in atogepant-treatment patients at 6% (compared to 1% in placebo) and 7%
(compared to 3% in placebo), respectively. In addition, constipation and nausea were the most
common reasons for discontinuation in the placebo-controlled trials, although the absolute
number of patients who discontinued due to these events was small. In the long-term safety
studies, constipation was cited as a reason for discontinuation in one patient (0.08%), though
other terms in the gastrointestinal disorders category (including nausea, abdominal pain,
vomiting, constipation, and dyspepsia) were cited as AEs leading to discontinuation in a total of
13 patients (1.1%).
There is no apparent association with atogepant treatment and serious gastrointestinal events;
however, the risk of such events should continue to be evaluated in the postmarketing setting
through enhanced pharmacovigilance.
7.6.10.4. Suicidality
Suicidal ideation (SI) and suicidal behavior evaluations were conducted across all clinical trials
of atogepant (based on the recommendations to evaluate such risk in the development programs
77
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

for all drugs with central nervous system activity as described in the Guidance for Industry:
Suicidal Ideation and Behavior: Prospective Assessment of Occurrence in Clinical Trials 2). The
clinical trials excluded patients who had significant risk of self-harm based on clinical interview
or responses on the C-SSRS (type 4 or 5 on the C-SSRS) in the past 6 months or suicidal
behavior in the past 6 months. The C-SSRS has categories with binary responses (yes/no) with
Category 1 (“wish to be dead”) to Category 5 (“active suicidal ideation with specific plan and
intent”) and further categorizations with aborted, interrupted, actual and completed suicide
(Category 10).
Group 1 - Placebo-Controlled Studies
In Study CGP-MD-01, there was one patient with SI in the 30 mg BID arm (“wish to be dead”
with no specific plan or intent). No patients reported suicidal behavior.
In Study 3101-301-002, one patient in atogepant 10 mg arm and two patients in placebo arm
reported SI during the double-blind treatment period. No patients reported SI with intent to act.
One patient in the placebo arm discontinued the study due to SI with aborted attempt and this
patient is not reviewed here.
In total 2 out of 1,142 (0.2%) patients in atogepant-treated arms reported SI compared to 3 out of
408 (0.7%) in the placebo arms.
Group 2 - Long-Term Safety Studies
In the long-term safety study, nine patients (0.8%) in the atogepant 60 mg arm reported SI on the
C-SSRS assessment (three with “wish to be dead,” three with nonspecific suicidal thoughts, one
with active suicidal ideation with some intent to act without a specific plan, and one with a plan
and intent), and five patients (2.6%) in the SoC arm reported SI. In the safety follow-up period,
an additional three patients (0.3%) reported suicidal ideation in the atogepant 60 mg QD arm.
Suicidal behavior was reported on the C-SSRS assessment by two (0.2%) of atogepant-treated
patients during the open-label treatment (both were discontinued from the study) due to actual
attempts and one patient (0.5%) reported suicidal behavior in the follow-up period. Two of these
cases are described below:
Review of Cases With Suicidal Behavior
(b) (6)

(1) Patient
was a 28-year-old white female with a history of migraine, obesity,
anxiety, major depression, and post-traumatic stress disorder who was enrolled in Study
CGP-MD-01 (60 mg QD) and then rolled over to the long-term safety study. On Day 34
of treatment, she took 28 tablets of acetaminophen for abdominal pain and migraine, but
she denied suicidal ideation (though a friend thought it was an attempt to self-harm). She
was admitted for accidental overdose with acetaminophen. Study treatment was
temporarily discontinued, and she was treated for the acetaminophen overdose. On Day
37 that patient self-admitted for post traumatic reaction. The patient then entered the
long-term safety study and was placed on atogepant 60 mg daily. On Day 60 of the longterm safety study, she was started on escitalopram 5 mg daily for worsening depression.
She was reported to have sinus node dysfunction (tachy-brady syndrome) on Day 71 with

low blood pressure and was started on fludrocortisone. On Day 155, the patient had an

(b) (6)

(2) Patient
reported nonsuicidal self-injurious behavior, “active suicidal ideation
with specific plan and intent” and an actual attempt (SAE) and was discontinued from the

suicide attempts, depression, prior hospitalization for ecstasy use, epilepsy, and a family
history of schizophrenia and bipolar disorder per the SAE report. The patient had a
history of being abused, had five children, and reported that she was unemployed. On
Day 63 of treatment in the long-term safety study with atogepant 60 mg QD, she
experienced an SAE of suicide attempt (chronic major depressive disorder with suicidal
attempt and psychosis), contusion, and nausea. She reported taking 5 to 10 pills, but the
investigator noted that 16 pills were unaccounted for. She was admitted and atogepant
was discontinued. She was discharged from the hospital on Day 71. This is a highly
confounded case due to prior suicide attempts and psychiatric history, therefore, a
relationship of this event to atogepant use is unlikely.
Assessment of SI/Suicidal Behavior
In the placebo-controlled pivotal studies, there were infrequent reports of SI. There were several
reports of both SI and suicidal behavior in the long-term safety studies, including one active SI
with specific plan and intent, and three overdose attempts that reportedly did not have associated
SI, but were clearly overdose situations. All SI in atogepant-treated patients were in patients with
a medical history of psychiatric illness except for one patient who reported a nonserious TEAE
of SI that resolved. In the placebo-controlled trials, the rate of SI was higher in the placebo arm
than in the atogepant treatment arm, and all cases described are confounded, making it unlikely
that atogepant contributed to these events. Comorbid depression is common in migraineurs and
the risk of suicide or SI in patients with depression is higher than in the general population. This
reviewer recommends routine pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting to evaluate for
suicidality.
7.6.10.5. Pregnancy
Three patients in the safety population in Study CGP-MD-01 became pregnant during the study.
Two of the three patients delivered babies that were reportedly healthy (one patient received 27
days of treatment with atogepant 30 mg QD during the first trimester and a second patient ended
treatment with atogepant at Day 87 and became pregnant approximately 13 days after
discontinuing atogepant 30 mg QD), and the third patient elected to terminate the pregnancy. The
patient who terminated the pregnancy was a 19-year-old woman who received atogepant 60 mg
daily for 82 days with first trimester exposure. She elected to terminate the pregnancy on Day 96
with an SAE of abortion.
Two patients in Study 3101-301-002 became pregnant during the study. Both patients were
randomized to 10 mg daily, treated for 8 and 21 days, respectively, and reportedly gave birth to

79
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

full-term healthy infants. One patient who was a 29-year-old white female on atogepant 10 mg
daily experienced a ruptured ectopic pregnancy.
Medical Officer’s Assessment: No conclusions can be drawn from the limited information in this
application about atogepant and pregnancy outcomes. This reviewer recommends the standard
pregnancy registry and postmarketing pregnancy outcomes study to further assess the effect of
atogepant on pregnancy and the unborn fetus.

7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
7.7.1. Elevation of LFTs and CPK With Atogepant
Issue
Evaluate cases of elevated LFTs and CPK, specifically in the setting of an SAE in which
elevated LFTs and CPK were associated with a fatality.
Background
Two oral CGRP receptor antagonists discontinued development (i.e., telcagepant and MK-3207)
due to an association with DILI. In the Phase 3 studies with telcagepant, liver injury signals
occurred with a relatively short latency of seven days (Holland and Goadsby 2018). The
mechanism of liver injury for these agents was postulated to be predominantly the result of a
combination of bile acid accumulation (transport inhibition) and oxidative stress (reactive
metabolite formation), and less so from mitochondrial toxicity (Smith et al. 2020). In vitro and in
silico testing have led to the development of newer CGRP receptor antagonists with the
expectation that these agents would not be associated with hepatotoxicity.
At the time of this review, neither of the two recently approved small molecule drugs that act on
the CGRP pathway, ubrogepant and rimegepant, have included warnings of DILI based on either
the premarketing or postmarketing data.
Of specific interest related to these concerns was one fatal SAE in the development program for
atogepant in which a 26-year-old woman experienced elevated LFTs and CPK and an
influenza-like illness within 4 weeks of starting atogepant de novo in Study 3101-302-002
(long-term safety).
The DILI team of the Division of Hepatology and Nutrition was asked to review this fatal SAE,
as well as the entirety of the application, to evaluate whether there may be a signal for
hepatotoxicity with atogepant use (Dr. Paul Hayashi was the reviewer). The Division of
Anti-Infectives was also consulted.
Lastly, given the association of elevated CPK with the elevated LFTs, an analysis of any effect
of atogepant on CPK, and an evaluation of AE terms related to muscle injury, were also
conducted.

80
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Assessment
SAE of Specific Interest
(b) (6)

A 26-year-old black female (Patient
died 43 days after initiating treatment with
atogepant 60 mg daily de novo in the long-term safety study. On Day 2, she was started on
sertraline 50 mg daily. On Day 4, she reported a “mild influenza like illness” that left her
bedridden for several days with fever and myalgias that she treated with over-the-counter drugs,
including acetaminophen. She did not seek medical attention for this illness. This event
reportedly resolved on Day 8. On Day 29, she experienced elevated LFTs (up to 20X ULN for
AST) and CPK (200X ULN) without a reported increase in total bilirubin or changes in
coagulation tests. She had no associated signs or symptoms (specifically, no fever, myalgias,
abdominal pain, weakness, rash, or fatigue, and a normal physical and neurological exam).
Atogepant was discontinued, however her LFTs continued to trend upward (with ALT 205, AST
677, and CPK 38,607). She had been working with a personal trainer and was told to stop
exercising. On Day 42, the patient reported “feeling fine” per a MedWatch report. Her ALT and
AST had fallen by 50% and her CPK was in the 400s. On Day 43, the patient experienced chest
pain and became unresponsive. Emergency services was called by her family. She required
cardiopulmonary resuscitation on the way to the hospital, and was successfully resuscitated, but
subsequently went into cardiac arrest again and died that day. Autopsy report findings
determined that she died from Group A beta hemolytic streptococcal infection (toxic shock
syndrome) with the same organism growing from her spleen, lung, and blood. She was noted to
have elevated creatinine and CPK at death. The Applicant’s assessment was that this event was
not related to study treatment.
The DILI team considers it unlikely that this case of elevated transaminases is associated with
atogepant use and considers the presence of myositis or myopathy to be better explanations for
the elevated AST and ALT, given the extremely high CPK levels. They suggest that the
myopathy may have been related to sertraline or excess exercise. The patient’s CPK and liver
enzymes had fallen by greater than 50% before the onset of shock; therefore, the Division of
Anti-Infectives consultant considers it unlikely that the elevated LFTs had a direct relationship to
the development of this patient’s overwhelming group A streptococcal (GAS) infection and
death. Therefore, no clear relationship of atogepant to the elevated transaminases or the infection
was identified by these consultants.
The Division of Anti-Infectives Clinical Reviewer’s Assessment of this fatal case is extracted
from her review, as described below:
Despite this being an atypical case, this patient’s clinical history does appear to be consistent
with streptococcal toxic shock syndrome related to invasive group A streptococcal infection.
Per the autopsy report, postmortem cultures grew GAS from spleen, lung, and blood. There was
no mention of an overt skin or soft tissue infection or other potential nidus. Therefore, the fact
that lung cultures were positive supports GAS pneumonia as the most likely source. Although
GAS is an uncommon causative pathogen, most cases of GAS pneumonia are communityacquired and occur in immune competent hosts. GAS pneumonia also tends to be aggressive,
with approximately 80% of cases leading to bacteremia and a case fatality rate of 38% (Muller
et al. 2003).

81
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Of significance, this patient developed a flu-like illness on study Day 4 and was reportedly
“bedridden for several days with fever and myalgias.” However, she did not seek medical care
and presumably was not tested for influenza or other respiratory viruses. Patients with a recent
history of respiratory viral infection are at increased risk for bacterial superinfections, including
GAS (Muller et al. 2003). Although the five-week delay from the inciting viral illness in this case
may have been somewhat prolonged, she may still have been at increased risk for a secondary
bacterial pneumonia.
Approximately one third of invasive GAS infections lead to development of streptococcal toxic
shock syndrome (Svensson et al. 2000; Ekelund et al. 2005). Toxic shock syndrome is defined by
rapid onset (within hours) of shock/hypotension and multiorgan failure. The mortality rate for
toxic shock syndrome ranges from 30 to 70% despite aggressive treatment (Nelson et al. 2016).
This patient reportedly “felt fine” a day prior to onset of symptoms, but subsequently developed
chest pain and shortness of breath the next morning, with rapid clinical deterioration and
cardiac arrest within hours of symptom onset. Although this is an example of a severe case, it
could certainly be consistent with the typical clinical course of invasive GAS infection/toxic
shock syndrome.
Based on the patient’s clinical course, including improvement in liver tests prior to onset of
infection, lack of evidence of synthetic dysfunction, and potential pulmonary risk factors, it is
unlikely that the suspected drug-induced liver injury significantly contributed to her
overwhelming GAS infection and subsequent death.
Evaluation of LFTs
A review of LFTs (transaminases) in the atogepant safety database was conducted to evaluate
whether there may be a signal for hepatotoxicity with atogepant use.
Overall, there were no clear imbalances in elevation of ALT or AST between the placebo (1.8%)
and atogepant treatment arms (1.0%) in the placebo-controlled trials. There were also no cases
meeting Hy’s Law criteria. In addition, no clear differences in LFTs were observed in the longterm safety studies between the atogepant and SoC arms.
Despite the lack of imbalances in the elevations of LFTs between treatment arms in the
controlled trials, there were a total of 10 cases of liver injury identified by Dr. Hayashi in the
atogepant database (including the original submission and the 120-Day Safety Update) that were
designated as having evidence of a probable or possible relationship to atogepant use. Of the six
cases of at least possible DILI that were identified in the original application, he considered four
cases as possible, and two cases as probable. The two cases of probable DILI and one
representative case of possible DILI due to atogepant are summarized below.
In addition, the 120-Day Safety Update Report included five new cases of transaminase
elevations that Dr. Hayashi assessed as follows: one unlikely, one possible, and three probable
DILI due to atogepant. Of the four at least possible cases, one case occurred in a healthy
volunteer PK study (CGP-PK-14), two cases in a preventive treatment of CM open-label safety
study (Study 3101-306-002), and one case in Study 3101-302-002 (a long-term extension study
of a preventive treatment of EM study). These were reviewed in detail by Dr. Hayashi in the
addendum to his review. No cases met Hy’s Law criteria.
Review of the 120-Day Safety Update Report also introduced a concern that the risk of
developing transaminase elevations was higher for patients of Asian descent, since Study
82
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

3101-306-002, which was an open-label study of 113 Japanese patients, demonstrated a higher
rate of transaminase elevation than the overall rate observed in this application. Ultimately, the
DILI team concluded that the data do not warrant labeling of a difference in liver injury risk for
Asians. Although the ongoing open-label safety study of 113 Japanese patients (Study
3101-306-002) indicated a higher rate of transaminase elevation than the overall rate in the
NDA’s ISS, no such increase was seen for Asians in the ISS nor the randomized controlled trial
from which patient’s entered Study 3101-306-002. Therefore, the number of Asians in the ISS is
too low to draw conclusions. In addition, the randomized controlled trial had 248 Asians
randomized across three arms (placebo, atogepant 30 mg, and atogepant 60 mg), and in that
study only 1 out of 248 Asians had elevations in transaminases, which is unlikely to be
attributable to study drug. Given that the open-label safety study had no comparator, the DILI
team concludes that they cannot definitively assign an increased risk of DILI among Asians
based on the current data.
A summary of representative cases of DILI are as follows:
Probable DILI due to atogepant:
•

(b) (6)

Patient
in Study 3101-301-002 was a 39-year-old white female who had
elevated LFTs without jaundice 3 weeks after starting atogepant 30 mg QD. At baseline,
the patient was on acetaminophen but had normal liver function and CPK tests. She
developed elevated LFTs on a routine follow-up visit at 3 weeks but was asymptomatic.
Her ALT was 248, AST 107, AP 169, and bilirubin 0.2. Atogepant was held and her
LFTs normalized within 9 days. She was restarted on atogepant and her ALT and AST
rose to 96 and 55, respectively. She remained asymptomatic. Atogepant was stopped
again and follow-up labs 15 days later were normal. She was lost to follow-up thereafter.
Given that this patient had a positive rechallenge, a relationship of this event to study
drug seems probable.

Probable DILI due to atogepant:
•

(b) (6)

Patient
in Study CGP-MD-01 was a 39-year-old white female who developed
elevated transaminases without jaundice 12 days after starting atogepant 60 mg QD. The
patient had no history of pre-existing liver disease and drank one alcoholic beverage per
week. The patient had normal LFTs at baseline, with a slight increase at screening (only
slightly higher than 1X ULN). On Day 13, she had elevated ALT (10X ULN) and AST
was 7X ULN (with normal bilirubin and AP). The study drug was stopped 5 days after
onset of elevated LFTs. Viral hepatitis serology was negative, autoimmune hepatitis
markers were negative, and ultrasound was unrevealing. The patient was taking Excedrin
Migraine frequently (every other day) but precise dosing was not provided, and this drug
was continued through the washout after stopping study drug.
— Per Dr. Hayashi, this case is a weak probable DILI due to atogepant. The
acetaminophen is a confounder, but its use continued throughout the washout period.
The Applicant suggested this case represented nonalcoholic fatty liver disease, but
that disease is not typically associated with such an acute rise and fall of ALT. The
Applicant also noted that on the day of DILI onset (ALT reported as >300), the
patient took a double dose of atogepant. While a relationship of this event is probable,
one fact suggesting that this elevation may have been due to another underlying
etiology is the high/normal LFTs reported in this patient at baseline; however,

Qulipta (atogepant)

considering the rapid drop in the transaminase levels when the drug was stopped

Possible DILI due to atogepant:
•

(b) (6)

Patient
in Study 3101-302-002 was a 56-year-old white female who
developed elevated transaminases without jaundice 24 weeks after initiating treatment
with atogepant 60 mg QD. She was taking acetaminophen at baseline and AP was mildly
elevated (slightly greater than 1X ULN) at baseline, but her LFTs and CPK were normal.
She developed asymptomatic elevation in transaminase levels at 24 weeks, which peaked
to ALT 177 and AST 83 one week later. She did have a positive cytomegalovirus
immunoglobin M and a flu-like illness that came on after the transaminitis was noted.
She continued study drug and her liver enzymes decline by 50% from the peak level, but
her LFTs stayed elevated over baseline for weeks 22 to 27 on study drug. The LFTs
returned to normal 9 weeks after stopping atogepant.
— Per Dr. Hayashi, this may be a case of chronic DILI while on atogepant with partial
or slow adaptation; however, the 24-week onset (compared to the other probable DILI
case) is a long latency. Autoimmune hepatitis may have played a role but the fact that
the injury did resolve without treatment and after the drug was discontinued makes it
less consistent with a diagnosis of autoimmune hepatitis.

Evaluation of CPK
Given that there were several cases of elevated CPK in association with elevated LFTs, an
analysis of CPK-related findings (as a TEAE and as a laboratory abnormality) was conducted.
There have been no definite associations of elevated CPK values with the approved
drugs/biologics that act on the CGRP pathway to date.
As noted previously during the review of laboratory findings (Section 7.6.6), CPK results
fluctuated across all treatment arms, including placebo, with approximately 4% of both the
atogepant- and placebo-treated patients experiencing an increase in CPK of >3X ULN and 1%
with a CPK >10X ULN. There was no dose-related increase in CPK elevations.

This analysis did not indicate an increase in muscle injury or musculoskeletal AEs in the

to muscle injury or CPK elevations) and laboratory analyses of CPK elevations also did not
indicate an increase in these events in the atogepant-treated groups compared to placebo.
Conclusion
The analysis for liver injury demonstrates that there were several cases of transaminase
elevations in patients taking atogepant in clinical trials where the relationship to atogepant was
considered either probable or possible. No Hy’s law cases were identified, and there was no
imbalance of LFT abnormalities overall between atogepant and placebo-treated patients.
The Division of Hepatology and Nutrition DILI team concludes that there was no significant
liver toxicity that would threaten approval of atogepant. The fatal case did not involve DILI. The
team also notes that there were 10 DILI cases described in the atogepant safety database that
were assessed as having either a probable or possible relationship to atogepant use. All cases
were asymptomatic and resolved with discontinuation of atogepant. The DILI team recommends
language for lableing and also concluded the following:
(1) Routine blood tests for liver enzyme monitoring is not necessary.
(2) The use of atogepant should be avopided in patients with severe hepatic impairment.
Following labeling negiotiations, the following language for Section 6 of the Prescribing
Information was agreed-upon related to this issue:
In Study 1 and Study 2, the rate of transaminase elevations over 3 times the upper limit of
normal was similar between patients treated with QULIPTA (1.0%) and those treated
with placebo (1.8%). However, there were cases with transaminase elevations over 3
times the upper limit of normal that were temporally associated with QULIPTA
treatment; these were asymptomatic, and resolved within 8 weeks of discontinuation.
There were no cases of severe liver injury or jaundice.

7.7.2. Increased Ischemic Vascular Risk
Issue
Evaluate if there is an increased risk of vascular ischemia, specifically ischemic cardiovascular
events, hypertensive events, ischemic cerebrovascular events, or thrombotic events with the use
of atogepant.
Background
Theoretical safety concerns associated with CGRP inhibition include cardiovascular,
cerebrovascular, and peripheral vascular events. Specifically, CGRP is thought to play a role in
compensatory vasodilatation in the setting of tissue ischemia; therefore, there is a concern that
CGRP antagonism may increase the risk of ischemic events (Aradi et al. 2019; Mulder et al.
2020).
The prescribing information for erenumab (Aimovig), another drug that acts on the CGRP
pathway, was amended after initial approval to include hypertension in the Warnings and
Precautions section after postmarketing surveillance revealed an association between elevated
blood pressure and erenumab (Saely et al. 2021).
Assessment
Ischemic vascular events discussed in this section focus on ischemic cardiovascular disease,
hypertension, ischemic cerebrovascular disease, and thrombotic events. The general approach
provides an analysis of SAEs, AEs, discontinuations, and relevant narratives by safety signal,
followed by an overall assessment of the safety signal as it relates to atogepant use.
Ischemic Cardiovascular Disease
An analysis of AEs, SAEs, ECG changes related to ischemia, and discontinuations related to
cardiovascular risk was conducted to assess if there is an increased risk of cardiovascular
ischemic events with atogepant use compared to placebo in the pivotal trials (ISS Group 1) and
long-term safety studies (ISS Group 2).
Patients with cardiovascular risk factors at baseline were enrolled in the placebo-controlled trials.
A baseline history of hypertension was reported by approximately 10 to 11% of patients in each
arm across the placebo-controlled studies.
A search of the preferred term “ischemic heart disease” yielded no cases in any group for the
placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1) and long-term safety studies (ISS Group 2). There were
no discontinuations related to ischemic events.
No ECG changes seen in this application appeared to be related to cardiovascular ischemic
events. ECG changes not related to ischemic changes are discussed in Section 7.6.7of this
review.
86
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Medical Officer’s Assessment: Few cardiovascular events were reported across groups
(presented previously) and no dose-dependent relationship in the risk of these events was
evident. There were no ischemic heart disease events in the atogepant arms of the placebocontrolled or long-term safety studies. The data do not indicate an increased risk of ischemic
cardiovascular events with use of atogepant; however, enhanced pharmacovigilance in the
postmarketing setting is recommended. This approach is consistent with that taken for all of
approved drugs of this class, to date.
Cerebrovascular Ischemic Events
Cerebrovascular ischemic events were evaluated. There were few cerebrovascular events
reported across the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1) (Table 43).

(b) (6)

(2) Patient
was a 41-year-old white female with a history of migraine,
hyperlipidemia, obesity, and was on oral contraceptives (estrogen containing) who

treatment with atogepant 60 mg QD. This event resolved on the same day and was not

87
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Medical Officer’s Assessment: The first patient’s event of hemiparesthesia may have been
related to a transient ischemic attack, but this may have also been related to anxiety or a
complicated migraine. Another patient in this database, discussed previously, experienced an
SAE that may also have represented a transient ischemic attack. Both of these cases were
confounded by other concomitant diagnoses, but a relationship between atogepant use and these
possible cerebrovascular symptoms cannot definitively be ruled out.
The second patient’s transient vision loss may have been the result of ischemia, but it may also
have resulted from an aura occurring with the migraine. Given that a workup followed to
evaluate this patient for ischemia, it seems that transient ischemic attack was in the differential.
Since an MRI (~14 days later) did not show evidence of acute/subacute infarct, no conclusive
diagnosis can be made.
Overall, a relationship of these events to atogepant cannot be ruled out. Given the continued
uncertainty of the relationship of atogepant to possible cerebrovascular ischemic events, this
reviewer recommends enhanced pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting to evaluate for
this possible safety signal. This approach is consistent with that taken for all of approved drugs
of this class, to date.

1.0%, and 0.2% in the atogepant 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg arms, respectively. The narratives are
reviewed below. New antihypertensive medication use was examined by the Applicant and
occurred in 0.5%, 0.6%, 1.0% and 0% of patients in the placebo, 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg arms,
respectively.

changes over the course of the long-term safety studies did not reveal any changes or safety
concerns. Evaluation of patients based on predefined criteria for potentially clinically significant

Medical Officer’s Assessment: It is difficult to assess a causal association with study drug in
any of the cases with hypertensive events, since all the patients had a baseline or predose (i.e.,
prior to the first dose of atogepant) elevated blood pressure reading. Several patients had been
on antihypertensive agents or were started on treatment for high blood pressure during the trial.
Some patients had blood pressure readings normalize during continued treatment with atogepant
and others had continued slight elevations in blood pressure readings. There was no clear doserelated increase in high blood pressure as slight elevations occurred with most doses (ranging
from 10 mg QD to 30 mg BID to 60 mg QD, but none in the 60 mg BID arm).
Only one patient had study drug discontinued and she was not rechallenged. Given that all
patients had an elevated blood pressure reading at follow-up in the cases reviewed, and that the
vital sign outlier analyses of both systolic and diastolic blood pressure did not demonstrate an
imbalance in the atogepant-treated arms as compared to placebo arms, it is unclear at this time
if atogepant causes worsening hypertension in patients with baseline high blood pressure.
Therefore, the review of all the hypertensive events associated with atogepant use does not
indicate a clear safety signal; however, this reviewer recommends enhanced pharmacovigilance
in the postmarketing setting based on mechanistic plausibility and the occurrence of similar
events noted with at least one other drug that acts on the CGRP pathway. This approach is
consistent with that taken for other approved drugs of this class.
Thrombotic events
There were two patients in the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1) who experienced
thrombotic events. The narratives are provided below:

(b) (6)

Patient
was a 57-year-old white female with a history of migraine, peripheral
artery occlusion and peripheral artery stent who completed the pivotal study on atogepant

have an unclear association with atogepant although the postoperative case was most likely
procedure related. The event that occurred in the open-label safety study of worsening
peripheral vascular disease is confounded since the patient had underlying disease with a
history of a stent. It is unclear if atogepant contributed to this event since the patient had been on
a 10 mg dose for 3 months without a similar event and then transitioned to a higher dose when
the event occurred.
Overall, the data do not indicate an increase in the risk of thrombotic events in the atogepant
safety database.
Conclusion
Vascular risk related to atogepant was assessed by analyzing cardiovascular (ischemic) events,
hypertensive events, cerebrovascular ischemic events, and thrombotic events. The analysis of
SAEs, TEAEs, selected patient narratives, vital signs, and ECG data did not reveal an increase in
cardiovascular or cerebrovascular ischemic events related to atogepant use in this application.
There were several patients with baseline/predose high blood pressure readings who had
continued elevated blood pressure readings after treatment with atogepant. The safety data
provided in this application related to vascular risk do not support inclusion of language
regarding vascular risk in the label of atogepant. Enhanced pharmacovigilance in the
postmarketing setting for cardiovascular events, cerebrovascular events, and hypertension is
recommended by this reviewer.

7.7.3. Decreased Body Weight
Issue
Evaluate if decreased body weight is appropriate for inclusion in labeling.

91
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Background
The Applicant asserts that data from the atogepant development program demonstrate an
increased risk of weight loss with treatment that should be described in labeling; however, the
potential also exists that such a statement could also be viewed as promotional. Therefore, an
assessment of this risk was conducted along with a consideration of any appropriate related
description in labeling.
Assessment
As displayed in Figure 17, Dr. Bunner analyzed the mean change and percent change from
baseline for weight (kg) and BMI (kg/m2).
Figure 17. ISS Group 1: Mean Change in Weight (kg), Safety Population

Source: advs.xpt; Software: R
Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; ISS, Integrated Summary of Safety; QD, once daily

The mean change in weight across arms appeared to be greatest at the end of treatment visit
(Week 12) and demonstrated a dose-response, with doses up to 60 mg BID having the greatest
decrease in weight. With respect to the three daily doses, the 10 mg dose appeared to be
associated with an increase in weight of 0.09 kg compared to a decrease in the 30 mg QD
(weight loss of 0.22 kg) and 60 mg daily arms (weight loss of 0.94 kg) at the end of treatment
(12 weeks of placebo-controlled treatment).
To evaluate for a change that may be considered clinically meaningful, Dr. Bunner also
conducted an analysis to evaluate how many patients had experienced a ≥5% weight loss and
≥7% weight loss at any time during the study (Table 45). The ≥5% cutoff was chosen based on
the clinically meaningful threshold used in Prescribing Information (PI) for orlistat (Zenical)
(Cheplapharm) and phentermine (Qsymia) (Vivus), two drug products indicated for weight loss.
Furthermore, the ≥7% cutoff was chosen based on the PI for sertraline (Zoloft) (Pfizer) where
weight loss is described as a safety finding.

Weight Changes
Table 45 demonstrates that ≥5% and ≥7% weight loss occurred at any time during the study at
similar frequencies in the atogepant 10 mg and 30 mg arms and placebo arms; however, the
60 mg QD arm had a 13.4% incidence of ≥5% weight loss and a 4.7% incidence of ≥7% weight
loss, which is a 6.5% and 2% risk difference, respectively, from placebo. Across all arms, the
treatment difference for ≥5% weight loss was ~3% between all atogepant-treated and placebo
arms. Approximately 2/3 of patients who experienced ≥5% or ≥7% weight loss at any time, had
this weight loss demonstrated at the end of treatment (Week 12) visit. Treatments were
discontinued at this visit, so the end of study visit would represent 4 weeks off treatment. Almost
half of patients had the weight loss ≥5% sustained at the end of study visit.
Of the 157 experiencing ≥5% weight loss:
•
•
•

47 patients were normal weight (BMI ≤25)
54 patients were overweight (BMI 25 to ≤30)
56 patients were obese (BMI ≥30 [range 30 to 53.8])

An outlier analysis of weight gain was also conducted. The data (not shown) suggest that more
patients in the placebo arms gained weight compared to those on higher doses (≥30 mg daily) of
atogepant.
In the long-term safety study, weight loss ≥5% had an incidence of 30.9% in the atogepant
60 mg QD arm and 24.5% in the SoC arm. Of note, ~36% of patients in the SoC arm were taking
topiramate, which has an established relationship with weight loss. The maximum weight loss of
-1.7 kg (3.7 pounds) occurred at Months 6 to 9. The weight loss appeared sustained out to 12
months in the long-term safety study, though there was a two-thirds attrition of patients by the
end of the long-term safety study, limiting interpretability of these results.
There are no clear patterns to weight change, in terms of which patients were disproportionately
affected (higher or lower weight, gender, race, or age).
Associated symptoms and weight changes:
Given that nausea and decreased appetite were examined in association with weight changes and
that there was a relatively low frequency of any these preferred terms (e.g., nausea, decreased

Qulipta (atogepant)

appetite and reported weight loss), no associations could be drawn from these subgroup analyses
(not shown).
Conclusion
A mild degree of weight loss (mean weight loss of 0.43 kg [0.95 pounds] to 0.94 kg [2.1
pounds]) appears to be associated with use of atogepant 30 mg and 60 mg daily, respectively.
Across all arms of the placebo-controlled studies, 9.8% of atogepant-treated patients, compared
to 6.9% of placebo-treated patients experienced ≥5% weight loss. In addition, the proportion of
patients with a weight decrease ≥7% at any point was 2.8% for placebo, 3.8% for atogepant
10 mg, 3.2% for atogepant 30 mg, and 4.9% for atogepant 60 mg. Weight gain does not appear
to be associated with atogepant use.
Based on the review of weight changes in the placebo-controlled trials, this reviewer
recommends including the following statement in labeling:
(b)

The proportion of patients with a weight decrease (4)7% at any point was 2.8% for
placebo, 3.8% for atogepant 10 mg, 3.2% for atog pant 30 mg, and 4.9% for
60 mg.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Hepatic Impairment
In a dedicated hepatic impairment (HI) clinical study (CGP-PK-01) in subjects with mild (ChildPugh A), moderate (Child-Pugh B) and severe (Child-Pugh C) HI, the total atogepant area under
the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC) increased by 24%, 15%, and 38%, respectively
relative to healthy controls. Based on the magnitude of impact of HI on the total atogepant
(b) (4)
exposures, the Applicant proposed
The protein binding data collected at predose and 2 hours postdose from the HI study indicated
that the subjects with normal hepatic function, mild, moderate, or severe HI showed varied free
fraction (% unbound) of atogepant at 1.8%, 2.6%, 2.9%, and 4.7%, in human plasma,
respectively. Based on protein binding data collected from all the subjects, the unbound
atogepant AUC increased to 1.84, 1.91, and 3.66-fold in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe
HI relative to healthy controls, respectively. Based on the absorption, distribution, metabolism,
and excretion properties, atogepant is likely to be considered as a low hepatic extraction drug and
the clinical significance of changes in unbound concentrations of atogepant is not clear. In
addition, the analysis on unbound atogepant PK has few limitations, e.g., plasma protein binding
data were collected at limited time-points to derive unbound exposures, and thus the
characterization of unbound atogepant PK should be interpreted as an exploratory assessment
(refer to Section 14.2, Study CGP-PK-01 for additional details).
Based on the magnitude of impact of mild and moderate HI on total and unbound atogepant
exposures (i.e., less than 2-fold increase in AUC based on unbound concentrations noted above
as a worst-case scenario), the review team agrees with the Applicant’s proposal to not adjust
94
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

intermittent dialysis, we recommend dose adjustment to 10 mg QD. Because atogepant is a
highly protein bound drug (>95% protein binding), dialysis may not have a significant impact on
atogepant exposures. As a cautionary measure, the review team suggests taking atogepant after
dialysis session in such patients.
In conclusion, the review team agrees with the Applicant’s labeling proposal to have no dose
adjustment for patients with mild and moderate RI, and recommends a lower dose of 10 mg QD
in patients with CLcr less than 30 mL/min. For patients with ESRD undergoing intermittent
dialysis, the review team recommends taking atogepant 10 mg QD after dialysis.
Other Intrinsic Factors
The population PK analysis indicated that sex, race, age, and body weight do not have a
clinically relevant effect on atogepant PK. Therefore, no dose adjustments are warranted based
on these factors (Section 14.3).

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
8.2.1. Food Effect
Study 3101-105-002 evaluated the impact of a high-fat meal (total calories of approximately 800
to 1,000, derived approximately 150 calories from protein, 250 calories from carbohydrates, and
500 to 600 calories from fat) on atogepant PK. The geometric mean values of AUC and Cmax
were reduced by 18% and 22%, respectively, while the median time to maximum concentration
was unaffected. Atogepant was administered without regard to food in Phase 2 and 3 studies
(CGP MD-01 and 3101-301-002). Since administration of atogepant with a high-fat meal did not
reveal clinically relevant effects on atogepant PK, the review team agrees with the Applicant’s
proposed labeling that atogepant can be taken without regard to food-intake (see Section 14.2).

8.3.1. Effects of Other Drugs on Atogepant
Atogepant is predominately metabolized by CYP3A4 through oxidation. Atogepant is a substrate
of P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporting
polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, OATP1B3, and OAT1 transporters.

96
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

The Applicant investigated the effects of a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, a strong CYP3A4 inducer,
an OATP inhibitor, and a P-gp inhibitor on atogepant PK in clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI)
studies. The Applicant also evaluated the effects of a moderate or weak CYP3A4 inhibitor, and a
BCRP inhibitor on atogepant PK via PBPK modeling and simulation.
CYP3A4 Inhibitors
In a clinical DDI study (CGP-PK-02), coadministration of atogepant with itraconazole, a strong
CYP3A4 inhibitor, resulted in a 5.5-fold increase in atogepant AUC0­inf and a 2.2-fold increase in
Cmax. Atogepant dose should be reduced to 10 mg when administered concomitantly with strong
CYP3A4 inhibitors (see Section 14.2).
The Applicant used a PBPK model to predict the effects of moderate and weak CYP3A4
inhibitors on atogepant PK. The PBPK model predicted that concomitant administration of
60 mg atogepant with fluconazole, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, would result in increased
AUC and Cmax by 68% and 21%, respectively. Concomitant administration with cimetidine, a
weak CYP3A4 inhibitor, is predicted to increase AUC and Cmax by 8% and 5%, respectively.
The review team agrees with the Applicant’s PBPK prediction for effects of concomitant
administration of fluconazole and cimetidine on atogepant PK and these changes are not
considered to be clinically relevant. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed with concomitant
use of moderate or weak CYP3A4 inhibitors (see Section 14.4).
CYP3A4 Inducers
In a clinical DDI study (CGP-PK-12), coadministration of atogepant after multiple doses of
rifampin resulted in a 60% reduction in atogepant AUC0­inf and a 30% reduction in Cmax.
Rifampin, in addition to its effects in inducing CYP3A4, also affects other transporter pathways
such as OATPs, P-gp etc. As noted above, atogepant is a substrate of CYP3A4, OATP1B1/1B3
and P-gp. Therefore, the impact of concomitant administration of atogepant after multiple doses
of rifampin is likely a composite interaction mediated by induction of CYP3A4, inhibition of
OATP1B1/1B3, induction of OATPs, and induction of P-gp. Although the induction potential of
rifampin on OATP1B1/1B3 cannot be ruled out, there is uncertainty on the magnitude of the
impact of OATP1B1/1B3 induction on atogepant exposures. The review team conducted PBPK
simulations for multiple scenarios to evaluate the impact of different combinations of DDI
mechanisms on atogepant PK after multiple doses of rifampin. These simulations indicated that
the net effect of rifampin (at steady state) on OATP1B1/1B3 is not expected to be significant.
PBPK simulations on rifampin’s impact on CYP3A4 induction alone indicated that atogepant
AUC ratio (relative to controls) was 0.63 (refer to Section 14.4). Given the lack of clear
dose-exposure-response relationship for efficacy in the dose range of 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID,
the review team recommends adjusting atogepant doses to 30 mg or 60 mg with concomitant use
of strong CYP3A4 inducers. (see Section 14.4).
Coadministration of atogepant with moderate CYP3A4 inducers was not evaluated. The
magnitude of interaction with concomitant administration of moderate CYP3A4 inducers on
atogepant is expected be lower than that noted with strong CYP3A4 inducers. Therefore, the
review team recommends the same dose adjustment strategy when administering atogepant with
moderate CYP3A4 inducers as with strong CYP3A4 inducers.

97
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Coadministration of atogepant with weak CYP3A4 inducers was not evaluated. According to the
FDA Guidance for Clinical Drug Interaction Studies, 3 a weak inducer is expected to decrease the
AUC of a sensitive index CYP substrate by ≥20% to <50%. These changes are not considered to
have a clinically relevant impact because of a lack of a clear dose-/exposure-response
relationship for efficacy in the range of 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID. Therefore, no dose adjustment
is needed when coadministering weak CYP3A4 inducers.
OATP, P-gp, and BCRP inhibitors
In a clinical DDI study (CGP-PK-12), coadministration of atogepant with a single dose of
rifampin resulted in a 2.9-fold increase in atogepant AUC0­inf and a 2.2-fold increase in Cmax.
When rifampin was administered at single dose, rifampin’s effect on atogepant PK is likely to be
predominately mediated by inhibition on OATP (refer to Section 14.4). Therefore, the review
team recommends adjusting atogepant doses to 10 mg or 30 mg with concomitant use of OATP
inhibitors (see Section 14.2).
In a clinical DDI study (3101-103-002), coadministration of atogepant with quinidine, a P-gp
inhibitor, resulted in a 1.3-fold increase in atogepant AUC0­inf and a 1.04-fold increase in Cmax.
Results of PBPK simulations showed that coadministration of atogepant with a BCRP inhibitor
would result in a 1.2-fold increase of atogepant AUC0­inf and a 1.3-fold increase in Cmax.
Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering with P-gp and BCRP inhibitors.
Other Drugs for Migraine Treatment
In clinical DDI studies conducted with commonly coadministered drugs for migraine treatment,
e.g., sumatriptan (Study CGP-PK-13), naproxen (Study CGP-PK-06), and acetaminophen (Study
CGP-PK-06), no clinically relevant interactions were observed. Therefore, no dose adjustment is
needed when coadministering with sumatriptan, naproxen, and acetaminophen.
Drugs that Increase Gastric pH
In clinical DDI studies with esomeprazole (Study 3101-102-002), a proton pump inhibitor, and
famotidine (Study MK-8031 P008), a H2 receptor blocker, no clinically relevant interactions
were observed. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering with pH-altering
agents.

8.4. Effects of Atogepant on Other Drugs
8.4.1. Oral Contraceptives
In a clinical DDI study (Study MK-8031-P005), coadministration of multiple doses of atogepant
with a combined oral contraceptive containing ethinyl estradiol (EE) and levonorgestrel (LNG)
did not result in clinically relevant changes in EE or LNG exposures (Cmax and AUC). Therefore,
no dose adjustments in combined oral contraceptives are needed when coadministered with
atogepant.

8.5. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The initial approval of atogepant for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine with be
limited to adults. This development program triggers the Pediatric Research and Equity Act,
therefore pediatric postmarketing studies to evaluate safety and efficacy of atogepant in pediatric
migraine patients will be required. Please refer to Section 22 of this review for details.

8.6. Pregnancy and Lactation
Due to the lack of information on pregnancy and lactation outcomes for atogepant-treated
patients, postmarketing studies will be required for a pregnancy registry study and a pregnancy
outcomes study. Please see Section 22 of this review for details.
Oral administration of atogepant (0, 5, 20, or 125 mg/kg/day) to male and female rats prior to
and during mating and continuing in females to Gestation Day 7 resulted in no adverse effects on
fertility or reproductive performance. Plasma exposures (AUC) at the highest dose tested
(125 mg/kg/day) were approximately 15 times that in humans at the maximum recommended
human dose (MRHD) of 60 mg/day.
Oral administration of atogepant (0, 5, 15, 125, or 750 mg/kg/day) to pregnant rats during the
period of organogenesis resulted in decreases in fetal body weight and skeletal ossification at the
two highest doses. At the no-effect dose (15 mg/kg/day) for adverse effects on embryofetal
development in rats, plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 4 times that in humans at the
MRHD.
Oral administration of atogepant (0, 30, 90, or 130 mg/kg/day) to pregnant rabbits during the
period of organogenesis resulted in an increase in fetal visceral and skeletal variations at the
highest dose tested, which was associated with minimal maternal toxicity. At the no-effect dose
(90 mg/kg/day) for adverse effects on embryofetal development in rabbits, plasma exposure
(AUC) was approximately 3 times that in humans at the MRHD.
Oral administration of atogepant (0, 15, 45, or 125 mg/kg/day) to rats throughout gestation and
lactation resulted in decreased pup body weight at the highest dose, which persisted into
adulthood. At the no-effect dose (45 mg/kg/day) for adverse effects on pre- and postnatal
development, plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 5 times that in humans at the MRHD.

III. Appendices
12. Summary of Regulatory History
On June 5, 2012, Merck Sharp and Dohme Corp., a subsidiary of Merck and Co., Inc., submitted
investigational new drug (IND) application 114780 to study MK-8031 (atogepant) for the acute
treatment of migraine. Initial Agency reviews described MK-8031 as a selective antagonist of the
neuropeptide transmitter, calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP).
The Division of Neurology Products concluded that the Sponsor may proceed with its proposed
clinical investigation for the treatment of migraine on July 5, 2012. The Division determined that
safety and tolerability, as the primary endpoint, should be assessed based on clinical evaluation
with monitoring of elevation of hepatic transaminases, mainly serum alanine aminotransferase as
increases in alanine aminotransferase have been observed with a number of CGRP receptor
antagonists.
In an email dated February 11, 2013, the Sponsor notified the Division of its intent to develop
MK-8031 for the treatment of migraine prophylaxis, rather than for the acute treatment of
migraine.
On August 24, 2015, the application underwent a change of sponsorship from Merck Sharp and
Dohme Corp. to Aptalis Pharma Ltd. On July 21, 2016, the Division was notified of a change in
Sponsor from Aptalis to Allergan Pharmaceuticals International Limited, with Allergan Sales,
LLC. as the Sponsor’s United States Agent contact.
The Sponsor submitted Special Protocol Assessment (SPA) requests for its proposed rat
(CGP-TX-06, SPA-2) and mouse (CGP-TX-05, SPA-3) carcinogenicity studies on March 10,
2017. An agreement letter was issued for the SPA-2 and a no agreement letter for SPA-3 on
April 14, 2017. The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee was unable to concur on
doses for females because the 3-month study did not identify a maximum tolerated dose or a
dose that produced plasma exposures (area under the plasma concentration-time curve, AUC)
25-fold above that at the maximum human dose.
A Type C guidance (teleconference) meeting between the Division and Allergan took place on
June 27, 2017, to discuss nonclinical and clinical development plans. The Sponsor’s intent for
this meeting was to obtain feedback from the Division regarding the adequacy and potential for
its planned study, Study CGP-MD-01, titled “A Phase 2/3, Multicenter, Randomized,
Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Parallel-Group Study to Evaluate the Efficacy, Safety, and
Tolerability of Multiple Dosing Regimens of Oral AGN-241689 in Episodic Migraine
Prevention,” to be used as a pivotal trial. The Division stated that Study CGP-MD-01 was
inadequate to be a pivotal trial. At the time of this meeting, the proposed trial’s statistical testing
procedure allows for the possibility of statistical significance to occur at a low dose when a high
dose may not demonstrate stataistical significance. The Division stated that the results of the
study could be uninterpretable if the high dose does not meet statistical significance when the
low dose does. Additionally, the Division recommended that the primary endpoint be analyzed
based on a comparison of the change from baseline in the number of migraine headache days
over the entire double-blind treatment period rather than only the last four weeks of the treatment
period, as proposed. The Division advised Allergan to provide justification for excluding patients

13. Pharmacology Toxicology: Additional
Information and Assessment
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted
Under the Investigational New Drug
Application
13.1.1. Pharmacology
Atogepant is a calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist, with activity at
human and monkey CGRP receptors at low picomolar concentrations, and mouse, rat, rabbit, and
dog CGRP receptors at low nanomolar concentrations. In vivo, atogepant inhibited capsaicininduced dermal vasodilation up to 90% at nanomolar concentrations in monkeys and
approximately 45% at micromolar concentrations in rat and rabbit.
In secondary pharmacology assays, atogepant demonstrated low micromolar binding affinity at
CGRP-related receptors (AMY1 and AMY3), but only weak binding affinity at 2 of 116
receptors, enzymes, and ion channel assays (half inhibitory concentration >20µM).

13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
A complete battery of safety pharmacology (CNS, cardiovascular, and respiratory) endpoints
was adequately assessed and showed no safety concerns.

13.1.3. PK/ADME
In rats and monkeys following IV or PO administration, the half-life was between 1 and 4 hours,
oral bioavailability was 27%, and plasma protein binding was 92%. 14C-atogepant (total
radioactivity) was eliminated in the urine (~9%), bile (~55%), and feces (~18%). Peak tissue
concentrations in rat were as follows (highest to lowest concentration): GI tract > liver > bile
duct > salivary gland (submaxillary). Oxidative metabolism primarily occurred by CYP3A4.
Atogepant was a substrate for membrane-bound efflux transporters P-gp and BCRP, hepatic
uptake transporters OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, and the renal uptake transporter, OAT1. There
were no major or unique circulating metabolites in humans.

13.1.4. General Toxicology
The toxicologic and toxicokinetic profile of orally administered atogepant was assessed in a
series of single and repeat dose studies in rats and monkeys up to 6- and 9-months’ duration,
respectively. At the high dose (HD) in the 14-day (250 mg/kg) and 3-month (200 mg/kg)
toxicology studies in rat, there were body weight decreases of 23 and 16%, respectively, and
vacuolation in the small intestine. In the 3-month rat (10/sex/group) study (0, 5, 20, or
200 mg/kg), the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was the mid dose (MD) based on a
16% decrease in body weight gain at the HD. In the pivotal 6-month study in rat (15/sex/group;
0, 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg), there were non-adverse findings consisting of decreases in body weight
of up to 10% at the HD, which correlated with a decrease in food consumption. These findings
partially resolved during the recovery period. There was also minimum to marked vacuolation in
104
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

the villous epithelium of the small intestine in MD and HD animals, which resolved during the
recovery period. The HD was justified based on decreases in body weight gain at ≥200 mg/kg in
the 14-day and 3-month studies. The safety margin based on plasma AUC at the NOAEL of
100 mg/kg was 27 times relative to that at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of
60 mg. Plasma AUC0­t generally increased dose proportionally.
In the single dose (up to 1000 mg/kg) studies in monkey, vomiting was observed at 150 and
300 mg/kg and liquid feces was observed at 300 mg/kg. In the pivotal 9-month oral toxicology
study in monkey (4/sex/group, 0, 15, 40, or 300 mg/kg) there were no drug-related findings.
Plasma Cmax and AUC0­24 h increased dose proportionally from the low dose (LD) to MD and less
than dose proportionally from the MD to HD. The NOAEL was the HD. The study was
considered adequate based on the vomiting observed at the 150 and 300 mg/kg doses in the
single dose studies. At the HD (300 mg/kg) in the 9-month study, the safety margin was 10-fold
compared to that at the MRHD based on plasma AUC at the NOAEL.

13.1.5. Genetic Toxicology
Atogepant was negative in Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development-compliant
Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assays.

13.1.6. Carcinogenicity
Based on the FDA statistical analysis of the 2-year mouse and rat carcinogenicity studies,
atogepant was not carcinogenic. The study in female mice was not adequate because the HD was
not adequately justified; it was not demonstrated to be a maximum tolerated dose or maximum
feasible dose and did not achieve plasma exposures at least 25-fold that at in humans at the
MRHD.

13.1.7. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
A fertility study in male and female rats (20/sex/group) administered oral doses of 0, 5, 20, or
125 mg/kg (QD) for 15 days prior to cohabitation, showed no effects on the testes, sperm
motility or number, pregnancy status, corpora lutea, uterine implants, live or dead fetuses, or
resorptions. The HD was justified based on decreases in body weight gain at ≥200 mg/kg in the
14-day and 3-month studies. No toxicokinetic data were collected. (The exposure data from the
rat embryofetal development study was used to calculate margins.) At the high dose, plasma
exposure (AUC) was approximately 15 times that in humans at the maximum recommended
human dose (MRHD) of 60 mg/day.
In a preliminary oral embryo-fetal development study in rat (12/group), animals were orally
administered doses of 0, 5, 20, 250, or 750 mg/kg (QD) on gestation days (GDs) 6 through 20.
Findings consisted of decreased fetal body weight (11-14%) at 250 and 750 mg/kg. There were
105
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

no effects on fetal viability, weight, sex ratio, and external or visceral morphology. In the pivotal
study, doses of 0, 5, 15, 125, or 750 mg/kg (PO) was administered QD (20/group) on GDs 6
through 20. There were no adverse findings in the dam. The fetal NOAEL was 15 mg/kg based
on increases in total resorptions and post-implantation loss (125 and 750 mg/kg) and incomplete
ossification (125 and 750 mg/kg). Exposure increased greater than dose proportionally between 5
and 125 mg/kg and decreased between 125 and 750 mg/kg. At the no-effect dose (15 mg/kg),
plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 4 times that at the MRHD.
In the preliminary embryofetal development study in rabbits (12/group), animals were orally
administered doses of 0, 15, 45, 100, or 325 mg/kg (QD) on GDs 7 through 20. There was a 6%
decrease in maternal body weight gain at the high dose. At necropsy (GD 28) corpora lutea,
gross morphology, fetal viability and weight, sex, and fetal external and visceral morphology
were assessed. At the HD, there were decreases in male and female fetal weight of 10 and 14%,
respectively, compared to control. In the pivotal study, oral doses of 0, 30, 90, or 130 mg/kg
(23/group) were administered daily on GDs 7 through 20 and the necropsy was conducted on GD
28. Maternal body weight loss (18%) was observed at the HD. The fetal NOAEL was the MD
based on an increase in skeletal and visceral variations at the HD. Plasma exposure (AUC) at the
NOAEL for adverse development effects was approximately 3 times that in humans at the
MRHD.
In the pre and postnatal development study, rats (21/group) were orally administered doses of 0,
15, 45 or 125 mg/kg (QD) from GD 6 through Lactation Day 20. The NOAEL was at the MD
based on a decrease (8-13%) in pup body weight that persisted into adulthood. Toxicokinetic
data were not collected. (The data from the rat embryofetal development study were used to
estimate plasma exposure at 45 mg/kg.) Plasma exposure (AUC) at the NOAEL for adverse
effects on pre- and postnatal development was approximately 5 times that in humans at the
MRHD.

13.2.4.2. Embryonic Fetal Development
13.2.4.2.1. Study TT #12-7061: Preliminary Oral EmbryoFetal Developmental Toxicity and Toxicokinetic
Study in Rats
Rats were orally administered MK-8031 (0, 5, 20, 250, or 750 mg/kg, QD, 12/group) from GDs
6 through 20. The animals were assessed for mortality, physical signs, body weights, food
consumption, and toxicokinetics. On GD 21, animals were euthanized and assessed for number
of corpora lutea and gross placental morphology. The following developmental parameters were
assessed: fetal viability, fetal weight, sex ratio, and external and visceral morphology. Maternal
body weight between GDs 6 and 18 was decreased up to 36% compared to control in the 250 and
750 mg/kg groups. Fetal weights were decreased 11 to 14% in the same groups. Exposure
(AUC0­24 h) increased greater than dose proportionally between 5 and 20 mg/kg and less than
dose proportionally between 20 and 750 mg/kg (Table 86).

13.2.4.2.3. TT #12-7071: Preliminary Oral Embryo-Fetal
Developmental Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in
Rabbits
Rabbits (12 females per group) were orally administered MK-8031 (0, 15, 45, 100, or
325 mg/kg/day) from GD 7 through 20. Animals were assessed for mortality, physical signs,
body weights, food consumption, and toxicokinetics. At the HD, from GD 6 to 21, there was a
6% decrease in mean maternal body weight gain, compared to control. AUC0­24 h increased
greater than dose proportionally from 15 to 100 mg/kg and decrease from 100 to 325 mg/kg
(Table 91).

On GD 28, a necropsy was conducted, and uterine contents were examined for corpora lutea,
gross morphology, fetal viability and weight, sex ratios, and fetal external and visceral
morphology. In the HD group, there was a decrease in male and female fetal mean body weight
of 10 and 14%, respectively, compared to control.

Qulipta (atogepant)

13.2.5. Special Toxicology Studies
Computational modeling of in vitro experiments assessing bile acid transporter inhibition,
mitochondrial dysfunction, and oxidative stress suggested atogepant may have a reduced risk of
causing hepatotoxicity in humans compared to the predecessor small molecule CGRP receptor
antagonists, telcagepant and MK-3207.
A 3-day phototoxicity study of atogepant (up to 40 mg/kg/day followed by a single 90-minute
ultraviolet ray exposure) in 8 female pigmented rats showed no drug-related skin reactions or
ocular findings.

14. Clinical Pharmacology: Additional
Information and Assessment
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
The binding of atogepant to human plasma proteins was determined by equilibrium dialysis. In
Study MK-8031 PK003, atogepant at 0.1, 1, and 10µM was incubated for 4 hours in human
plasma and the unbound fraction was determined. The mean unbound fraction ranged from 4 to
6% across all atogepant concentrations.
In clinical Study CGP-PK-01, plasma protein binding was determined by equilibrium dialysis
using plasma samples collected from subjects with different degrees of hepatic impairment. In
participants with no, mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment administered a single oral
dose of 60 mg atogepant, the percentage of unbound atogepant was found to be 1.8%, 2.6%,
2.9%, and 4.7%, respectively.

14.1.2. Metabolism of Atogepant
In Study MK-8031 PK003, the in vitro metabolism of [14C]-atogepant was assessed using rat,
monkey, and human liver microsomes and mouse, rat, monkey, and human hepatocytes to
compare species-specific metabolism. Metabolites M1 (N-oxide on the azaoxindole moiety) and
M2 (5-hydroxylation on the azaoxindole moiety) were found following liver microsome
incubation. All human metabolites were observed in mouse, rat, and/or monkey hepatocytes, the
preclinical toxicology species.
In study MK-8031 PK007, circulating metabolites of atogepant in human plasma were
characterized using pooled clinical samples from Study MK-8031 P002. With a
semi-quantitative Liquid Chromatography-High Resolution Mass Spectrometry method, results
showed that atogepant was the predominant circulating component (97.8% of total exposure) and
metabolite M1 was a minor circulating metabolite (2.2% of total exposure) with no apparent
accumulation following multiple doses of atogepant. M2 was not found in the pooled clinical
samples.
In the clinical mass balance study of Study CGP-PK-03, the only two radiometric peaks detected
in plasma were atogepant and metabolite M23, together representing 15.3% of the circulating
radioactivity exposure. Characterization of M23 in Study 3101-N02-050 indicated that M23 is
143
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

not an acyl-glucuronide metabolite. Following a single dose of 50 mg atogepant, the AUC of
M23 was found to be 3 times lower than that from monkeys dosed with a single dose of
atogepant at 10 mg/kg in Study PK002.
In study MK-8031 PK003, cytochrome P450 (CYP) reaction phenotyping was conducted using
human liver microsomes incubated with inhibitory antibodies for CYP3A4/5, 2C, and 2D6 and
with recombinant CYP enzymes including CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and
CYP3A4. The results showed that the oxidative metabolism of atogepant occurred almost
predominantly via CYP3A4 with minor involvement of CYP2D6.

14.1.3. Inhibition and Induction of CYP Isozymes by
Atogepant
Results from in vitro studies showed that atogepant is not anticipated to inhibit CYP enzymes,
monoamine oxidase A, or UGT1A1.
Table 99. Studies MK-8031 PK003, 110337: Effect of Atogepant on Cytochrome P450 Marker
Enzyme Activities in Human Liver Microsomes

In Study PK003, atogepant was found to have no inhibition potential on monoamine oxidase A
and UGT1A with a half maximal inhibitory concentration of >122µM and 83µM, respectively.
In Study PK003, atogepant up to 20µM was found to have no induction potential on CYP1A2 or
CYP2B6. At 5 to 20µM, atogepant was found to increase by more than 2-fold the messenger
RNA activity of CYP3A4 compared to the vehicle control. However, atogepant is not expected
to have induction potential on CYP3A4 at clinically relevant concentrations.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
Mass Balance of Atogepant Following Administration of [14C]-Atogepant
(Study CGP-PK-03)
Study Design
Under fasting conditions, healthy male subjects (N=6) were administered 50 mg total (~200 µCi)
of a 14C-atogepant oral solution.
Blood samples were collected at predose through 168 hours, and up to Day 29, to measure total
radioactivity, atogepant concentration, and metabolite profiling. Urine and fecal samples were
collected to measure the levels of atogepant and metabolites, and total radioactivity from predose
through up to Day 29, if discharge criteria were met. If discharge criteria were not met, urine and
fecal sampling continued beyond Day 29 postdose.

146
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Results and Conclusion
•

Based on the AUC ratio of atogepant and total radioactivity in plasma, atogepant
contributed approximately 75% of the total radioactivity exposure and metabolite M23
contributed approximately 15%.

•

The total recovery of radioactivity in urine and feces 336 hours after dosing was
approximately 89%, with 8% from urine, and 81% from feces.
— In urine, atogepant represented approximately 5% of the radioactive dose and was the
only peak detected that was greater than 1% of the radioactive dose
— In feces, atogepant represented approximately 42% of the radioactive dose. At least
11 metabolites were detected in feces and each represented less than 10% of the
radioactive dose.

Reviewer’s Comments: The reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s assessments.

Food Effect of Atogepant (Study 3101-105-002)
Study Design
The study was a randomized, open-label, single-dose, 2-period crossover study conducted in 20
healthy subjects.
Intervention A: Single dose of 60 mg atogepant, hot melt extrudate oral compressed tablet
(HME-OCT) formulation, following a high fat and high calorie meal (with a total
of approximately 800 to 1,000 calories; approximately 150 calories derived from
protein, 250 from carbohydrates, and 500 to 600 from fat) consumed within 30
minutes before dosing.
Intervention B: Single dose of 60 mg atogepant, HME-OCT formulation, following a 10-hour
overnight fast.
The pharmacokinetics (PK) blood samples were collected starting on Days 1 (Period 1) and 8
(Period 2); at 0 hour (predose) and 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24, 36, and 48 hours postdose.

Conclusion
A high-fat, high-calorie meal reduced the atogepant AUC and maximum concentration (Cmax) by
18% and 22%, respectively, while the median time to maximum concentration was unaffected.
These changes in exposures are not expected to be clinically relevant.
Reviewer’s Comments: The reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s assessments.

Atogepant PK in Subjects With Hepatic Impairment (Study CGP-PK-01)
Study Design
Study CGP-PK-01 was a multicenter, nonrandomized, open-label, parallel-group, single-dose
study. Participants with normal hepatic function (healthy controls) and subjects with hepatic
impairment (mild, moderate, or severe according to the Child-Pugh classification) received a
single, oral, 60 mg tablet of atogepant under fasting conditions. A total of 32 subjects were
enrolled with 8 subjects for each group based on the hepatic function.
PK blood samples were collected at predose through 72 hours postdose. Plasma samples were
also collected for evaluation of protein binding at predose and two hours postdose.

respectively, in subjects with no, mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment.
Reviewer’s Comments: The protein binding data obtained from predose and 2 hours postdose
subject samples from study CGP-PK-01 showed minimal difference in atogepant protein binding.

Based on the unbound atogepant % (calculated as: 1-%bound) at 2 hours postdose and PK

comparison of unbound plasma atogepant PK exposures between subjects with mild, moderate,
or severe hepatic impairment and those with normal hepatic function. The results showed that
the exposure of unbound atogepant increased by 84%, 91%, and 266% in subjects with mild,

normal hepatic function. However, there are two limitations with the analysis on unbound
atogepant PK: 1) the method validation for the equilibrium dialysis assay using the rapid
equilibrium dialysis was not validated for protein binding analysis of samples from CGP-PK-01;
and 2) unbound atogepant PK was characterized based on the protein binding data obtained
from two time points, predose and 2 hours postdose, instead of multiple time points covering the
PK profile. Therefore, the unbound atogepant data should be interpreted as supportive
information.

Drug-Drug Interaction: Effect of Itraconazole on Atogepant PK (Study CGPPK-02)
Study Design
Study CGP-PK-02 used a fixed-sequence, open-label, single-sequence design to assess the effect
of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects. A 7-day
washout period was used between treatments.
Treatment A: Single oral 60 mg dose of atogepant under fasted conditions on Day 1.
Treatment B: On Days 8 to 14, 200 mg itraconazole was taken orally once daily under fed
conditions; on Day 15, 200 mg itraconazole was co-administered with 60 mg
atogepant under fasted conditions; on Days 16 to 17, 200 mg itraconazole was
taken once daily under fed conditions.
PK blood samples were collected at predose through 72 hours postdose on Day 1 and Day 15 to
determine atogepant concentration in plasma. Blood samples were also collected on Day 1 at 2,
4, 8, 12, and 24 hours postdose to compare atogepant concentrations determined in Dry Blood
Spot analysis. Blood samples were taken at predose on Day 8, 13, 14, and 15 to determine
itraconazole plasma concentration.

Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Results
Table 106. PK Parameters of Atogepant Following Treatment A and Treatment B in Healthy
Subjects

Source: Study CGP-PK-01, Table 11-1

Conclusion
The Cmax and AUC of atogepant increased by 2.15-fold and 5.5-fold, respectively, following coadministration of atogepant with itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor.
Reviewer’s Comments: The reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s assessments.

Drug-Drug Interaction: Effect of Single and Multiple Dose Rifampin on PK of
Atogepant (Study CGP-PK-12)
Study Design
Study CGP-PK-12 was an open-label, 2-treatment, 2-period, single-sequence, nonrandomized,
crossover interaction study conducted in healthy subjects. Rifampin is an inducer of both
CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein (P-gp), and an inhibitor of organic anion transporting polypeptides
(OATPs). All subjects received the following treatments with a sequence of A, B1, and B2 as
described below:
Treatment A (atogepant alone): A single dose of 60 mg atogepant was administered orally on
Day 1.
151
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Treatment B1 (Co-administration of atogepant with a single dose of rifampin): A single dose of
60 mg atogepant was co-administered with 600 mg rifampin (2 × 300 mg capsules) on Day 7,
followed by 600 mg once daily, rifampin alone, on Days 8, 9, 10, and 11.
Treatment B2 (Co-administration of atogepant after multiple doses of rifampin): A single dose of
60 mg of atogepant was co-administered with 600 mg rifampin on Day 12, followed by 600 mg
rifampin alone on Day 13.
On Days 1, 7, and 12, atogepant, either alone or co-administration with rifampin, was
administered under fasting conditions. When rifampin was administered alone (on Days 8, 9, 10,
11, and 13), subjects were not allowed to take food for 1 hour before and 1 hour after dosing.
Blood samples were collected at predose through 48 hours and postdose on Days 1 and 12. On
Day 7, blood samples were collected at predose through 24 hours postdose. Blood samples at
predose were collected on Days 11 and 12 to determine rifampin concentration.
