Qulipta (atogepant)

Conclusion
Co-administration of a single dose of atogepant with a single dose of rifampin resulted in
increases in atogepant Cmax and AUC by 2.85-fold and 2.23-fold, respectively.
Co-administration of a single dose of atogepant after multiple doses of rifampin resulted in
decreases in atogepant Cmax and AUC by 30% and 60%, respectively.
Reviewer’s Comments: The drug-drug interaction (DDI) of rifampin with atogepant is mediated
via multiple mechanisms. When rifampin was administered as a single dose, rifampin’s effect on
atogepant PK is likely to be predominantly mediated by inhibition of OATP. When rifampin was
administered at multiple doses, rifampin’s effect on atogepant PK is likely a composite
interaction mediated by induction of CYP3A4, inhibition of OATPs, induction of OATPs, and
induction of P-gp. Refer to Sections 8.2 and 14.4 for detailed discussion. Statistical comparison
of atogepant PK exposure between Treatment B1 and Treatment A did not include the statistical
analysis for AUC0­inf. Since AUCextrapolated contributed to only a small percentage (less than 5%),
the conclusions based on the AUC0­24h values are acceptable.

14.3. Pharmacometrics Review
14.3.1. Summary of Findings
14.3.1.1. Key Review Question: Do Any of the Factors,
Including Age, Renal Function (Mild and Moderate
Renal Impairment), Sex, Race, and Body Weight,
Affect the Pharmacokinetics Profile of Atogepant?
No. Based on population PK analysis, none of the factors, including age, renal function (mild
and moderate renal impairment), sex, race, and body weight, affect the pharmacokinetics profile
of atogepant.

14.3.2. Label Statements
8.5 Geriatric Use
Population pharmacokinetic modeling suggests no significant pharmacokinetic differences
between elderly and younger subjects. Clinical studies of QULIPTA did not include sufficient
numbers of patients aged 65 years and over to determine whether they respond differently from
younger patients. In general, dose selection for an elderly patient should be cautious, usually
starting at the low end of the dosing range reflecting the greater frequency of decreased hepatic,
renal, or cardiac function, and of concomitant disease or other drug therapy.
12.3 Specific Populations
Patients with Renal Impairment
The renal route of elimination plays a minor role in the clearance of atogepant. Based on a
population pharmacokinetic analysis, there is no significant difference in the pharmacokinetics of
atogepant in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment (CLcr 30-89 mL/min) relative to
those with normal renal function (CLcr >90 mL/min). As patients with severe renal impairment
153
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

or end-stage renal disease (ESRD; CLcr <30 mL/min) have not been studied, use of the lowest
effective dosage of atogepant (10 mg) is recommended in those patients
Other Specific Populations
Based on a population pharmacokinetic analysis, age, sex, race, and body weight did not have a
significant effect on the pharmacokinetics (Cmax and AUC) of atogepant. Therefore, no dose
adjustments are warranted based on these factors.
Reviewer’s Comment: We agree with the label statement.

14.3.3. Pertinent Regulatory Background
Atogepant is a potent, selective, orally administered, CGRP receptor antagonist in development
for the preventive treatment of migraine in adults with <15 migraine days per month. The
proposed dose regimens are:
•

30 mg or 60 mg once daily

•

Severe renal impairment: 10 mg

•

End-stage renal disease: 10 mg once daily

(b) (4)

once daily

14.3.4. Results of Applicant’s Analysis
A dataset composed of 11,763 observations from 1,356 patients was used to develop the final
population pharmacokinetic (PopPK) model. The overall PK profile was best described by a
three-compartment model with a sequential zero-/first-order lag time absorption model and linear
elimination from the central compartment. Formulation, dose, food status, liver function,
concomitant medication and body weight were each found to have a statistically significant
influence on the atogepant PK.
Healthy volunteers and patients with migraine shared the same PopPK model parameters, except
for apparent clearance, which was 22.9 L/h in healthy volunteers, while it was 23.7% lower in
patients with episodic migraines (17.4 L/h). The apparent central volume of distribution was
86.1 L and blood concentrations were predicted to be 57.3% of those in plasma. Median terminal
elimination half-lives were comparable between healthy volunteers (8.3 hours) and patients
(8.5 hours); the overall median estimate was 8.5 hours. Approximately 69% of the orally
bioavailable dose was absorbed through a zero-order process. The derived first-order absorption
rate constant estimate was 2.48 1/h. The influence of covariates, including age, renal function,
sex, race, and body weight, were not clinically relevant. The tested continuous and categorical
covariates over Etas profiles were plotted in Figure 22 and Figure 23, respectively. Continuous
covariates, such as creatinine clearance and body weight, have no influence on the PK
parameters, including the inter-individual variabilities of bioavailability, apparent clearance),
inter-compartmental clearance, duration of zero-order absorption, apparent rapid peripheral
volume of distribution, and the inter-occasional variability of bioavailability (Figure 22). The PK
parameters after single dose and during steady state were simulated with the final PopPK model
(Table 109 and Table 110).

14.3.5. Reviewer’s Analysis
14.3.5.1. Introduction
Atogepant (also known as AGN-241689 and MK-8031) is a potent, selective, oral calcitonin
gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist being developed for preventive treatment of
migraine. The proposed dose regimens are: (1) 30 mg or 60 mg once daily for patients with
(b) (4)
normal renal function, or and mild to moderate renal impairment; (2) 10 mg
once daily
for patients with severe renal impairment; and (3) 10 mg once daily for patients with end-stage
renal disease.
To support the recommended dose regimen, the Applicant developed a population PK model.
The overall PK profile was best described by a three-compartment model with a sequential
zero-/first-order lag time absorption model and linear elimination from the central compartment.
The factors, including age, renal function (mild and moderate renal impairment), sex, and race,
were not significant covariates for PK parameters. Body weight was found to be statistically
significant for central volume of distribution, however, with a mild relationship. In this analysis,
the reviewers adopted the Applicant’s population PK model to estimate the individual atogepant
exposure at steady states and confirmed the Applicant’s findings.
The exposure-efficacy relationship was best described by a zero-inflated Poisson model. The
treatment effect was comparable and independent of atogepant exposures across the evaluated
dose groups ranging from 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID.
14.3.5.2. Objectives
The analysis objective was to evaluate the influence of age, renal function (mild and moderate
renal impairment), sex, race, and body weight, on the pharmacokinetics of atogepant

In conclusion, we agree with the Applicant’s conclusion that the influence of age, renal
impairment (mild and moderate), sex, race, and body weight on atogepant PK is not clinically
relevant.
Exposure-Response Analysis
The Applicant conducted exposure-response modeling analysis. A total of 6,471 monthly
migraine days observations from 1,668 subjects were used for model building. Individual
atogepant steady-state exposures for exposure-response modeling analysis were predicted using
the post hoc estimates from the final PopPK model. The change from baseline monthly migraine
days over average atogepant concentration plot suggested a flat exposure-response relationship
within the subjects exposed to atogepant (Figure 32). The migraine days data were well
described by a zero-inflated Poisson model. Typical exposure metrics were simulated and the
individual Cmax, AUC, average concentration, and daily time above specified threshold levels
were calculated for each patient in the Phase 2/3 study. These exposure summaries were used to
test the exposure-response modeling in the efficacy models. The introduction of a treatment
effect generally provided an improvement of the model. However, a model which described the
treatment effect as being of equal magnitude, irrespective of atogepant exposure, performed
better than any model that took individual exposure into account. The final model showed
relatively good predictive capability to the observed efficacy data (Figure 33).

14.4. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic
Modeling Review
Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically based
pharmacokinetics (PBPK) report, entitled “Development of a mechanistic physiologically based
pharmacokinetic (PBPK) model for atogepant and subsequent evaluation of drug-drug
interaction potential as a victim of CYP3A4- and transporter-mediated interactions in healthy
adult volunteers” for the following intended purposes.
•

To predict the effect of a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducer on the PK
of atogepant if only considering CYP-mediated DDI in healthy subjects.

•

To predict the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., fluconazole) and a weak
CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., cimetidine) on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects.

•

To predict the PK of atogepant with and without complete knockout of P-gp or breast
cancer resistance protein (BCRP) transporter function in healthy subjects.

The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the submitted PBPK reports and supporting
modeling files. FDA reviewers conducted additional PBPK analyses and concluded the
following:
•

The atogepant PBPK models were able to capture the observed atogepant PK following a
single oral dose (50, 60, 100, and 170 mg) and multiple oral dose administration (QD: 50,
100, and 170 mg; BID: 30 and 60 mg) in healthy subjects.

•

The atogepant PBPK models are adequate to predict the effect of fluconazole and
cimetidine on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects. The model predicted a 69% and
8% increase in atogepant AUC with fluconazole (loading dose of 400 mg followed by
200 mg QD) and cimetidine (400 mg BID), respectively.

•

Atogepant exposure may be reduced by approximately 50% if co-administered with a
strong CYP3A inducer but not an inducer of any transporter.

•

Atogepant exposure may be increased by approximately 140% if co-administered with a
strong CYP3A inhibitor but not an inhibitor of any transporter.

•

The DDI potential of atogepant with a selective P-gp or BCRP inhibitor was low.
170

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

14.4.1. Applicant’s PBPK Modeling Effort
PBPK software
Simcyp V17 (Simcyp Ltd, United Kingdom) was used to develop the atogepant PBPK models
and predict the PK of atogepant with and without CYP3A and transporter modulators.

Model development
Atogepant
The Advanced Dissolution Absorption and Metabolism model was used to model atogepant
absorption. Atogepant was rapidly absorbed following administration of the tablet formulation
with a time to maximum concentration of 1.00 to 1.50 hours under fasting conditions. Singleand multiple-dose PK data indicated that the AUC and Cmax of atogepant increased in a roughly
dose-proportional manner over the dose range of 1 to 170 mg in healthy subjects, suggesting
solubility does not limit the absorption in vivo over this dose range. Thus, the Applicant assumed
the drug is absorbed in the form of a solution. The atogepant jejunal passive permeability value
of 2.85 x 10-4 cm/s was derived from Caco-2 apparent permeability data. The kinetic parameters
for intestinal P-gp and BCRP were obtained from transport study data in Caco-2 and MDCKIIBCRP cell lines. Relative activity factor values for intestinal P-gp and BCRP were optimized
based on the clinical PK data (Study CGP-PK-12) and excretion data in feces (Study
CGP-PK-03).
The fraction unbound in plasma was estimated to be 0.047 and applied in all simulations
assuming albumin as the main binding protein. The blood to plasma ratio was 0.77. A full PBPK
model was used to simulate the distribution phase of atogepant PK profile. A steady-state
volume of distribution value of 0.306 L/kg with a ratio of total concentration of compound in the
tissue to total concentration of compound in the plasma at steady-state scalar of 0.403 was
optimized using clinical PK data (Study CGP-PK-12 control arm). Based on the in vitro
immunoinhibition, chemical inhibition, and phenotyping study results, all metabolism of
atogepant was assigned to CYP3A4 in the PBPK model. The hepatic intrinsic clearance value
(22.8 L/hr) was optimized based on clinical PK data (Study CGP-PK-12). Atogepant was
minimally excreted in urine, with only 5% of the total radioactivity excreted in urine as the
unchanged parent drug. In feces, 42% of the dose was recovered as parent drug due to
unabsorbed drug, intestinal secretion and biliary excretion or a combined effect of these
processes. Renal clearance (1.11 L/h) was obtained from study CPG-PK-03.
OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP were incorporated within the “permeability-limited liver”
model to simulate hepatic transport processes. The kinetic parameter values for hepatic P-gp and
BCRP, and the intrinsic clearance value for OATP1B1/1B3-mediated active transport processes
in the liver were defined based on the transport data in Caco-2 cells, MDCKII-BCRP and
MDCKII-control cells, and MDCKII-OATP1B3 and MDCKII control cells, respectively. The
relative expression factor values for hepatic OATP1B1, OATP1B3, P-gp, and BCRP were
obtained from experimental data for transporter abundance in in vitro cell culture systems and
liver tissue. Multiple transporter-related processes (gut secretion, hepatic uptake, and biliary
secretion) with CYP3A4-mediated metabolism and renal clearance components incorporated in
the model allowed recovery of the systemic PK and the amount of drug excreted unchanged in
urine (4.84%) and feces (42%).
171
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Victim Drug Models
The default PBPK models of itraconazole, rifampin, fluconazole, and cimetidine in Simcyp V17
were used for DDI prediction.
With respect to the interaction parameter values, the in vitro obtained itraconazole inhibitory
constant (Ki) values for P-gp and BCRP (Kishimoto et al. 2014) were scaled down 15-fold and
used in the simulations (itraconazole Ki values for P-gp and BCRP are 0.054 and 0.054µM,
respectively). The in vitro obtained hydroxyitraconazole Ki values for P-gp and BCRP (Lempers
et al. 2016) were scaled down 37-fold and used in the simulations (hydroxyitraconazole Ki
values for P-gp and BCRP are 0.135 and 0.324µM, respectively). In addition, in vitro obtained
hydroxyitraconazole Ki values for OATP1B1 and OATP1B3(Lempers et al. 2016; Vermeer et al.
2016) were scaled down 15-fold and used in the simulations (hydroxyitraconazole Ki values for
OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 are 0.016 and 0.007µM, respectively).

14.4.2. FDA’s Assessment
FDA agreed with the Applicant’s assessment and all metabolism of atogepant was assigned to
CYP3A4 in the PBPK model. Given the renal clearance of 1.11 L/h, the contribution of CYP3A
to the overall atogepant clearance is about 0.75. Using the default itraconazole PBPK model in
Simcyp, the model predicted a 140% increase in atogepant exposure (AUC ratio [AUCR] is
2.43) without considering the effect of itraconazole on transporters. As the contribution of
CYP3A to the total metabolism of atogepant has been confirmed using in vitro and in vivo data,
the observed DDI effect with itraconazole (AUCR of 5.5) is likely partially contributed by
nonCYP mediated DDI mechanisms. Since in vitro study showed that atogepant is an
OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP substrate, the Applicant conducted additional DDI simulations
by incorporating the inhibition potential of itraconazole on OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP as
described in the method section. The simulated AUCR with itraconazole is consistent with the
observed data as shown in Table 118. The reviewer agreed that the observed atogepant DDI
effect with itraconazole might be contributed by both CYP and nonCYP-mediated DDI
mechanisms.
Rifampin may cause DDIs via multiple mechanisms. In the PBPK analysis, the
rifampin-mediated CYP3A4 competitive inhibition, P-gp and BCRP inhibition, and
OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effects were incorporated in the model to simulate the effect of a
single dose of rifampin on the PK of atogepant. It was reported that the competitive inhibition of
CYP3A4 by rifampin has minimal impact on the drug PK in vivo (Varma et al. 2013; Asaumi et
al. 2019). The impact of P-gp inhibition by rifampin on atogepant PK was likely low given the
clinically insignificant exposure change of atogepant with quinidine (1.25-fold, Study
3101-103-002). Additionally, the Applicant’s worst-case scenario analysis showed that the DDI
potential of atogepant with BCRP inhibitors was weak. Therefore, the observed exposure change
of atogepant (AUCR 2.77) with a single dose of rifampin was most likely caused by the
inhibition of hepatic uptake transporters OATP1B1/1B3 by rifampin.
To simulate the impact of multiple doses of rifampin on the PK of atogepant, the
rifampin-mediated CYP3A4 induction, CYP3A4 competitive inhibition, and the P-gp induction
effect were incorporated in the default rifampin model. Intestinal P-gp induction by rifampin
(600 mg QD) was defined as a 3.5-fold increase in P-gp expression whereas the negligible
induction effect of rifampin (600 mg QD) on hepatic P-gp was assumed (Greiner et al. 1999).
172
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

The rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effect was not incorporated in the Applicant’s
model. The reviewer simulated the effect of multiple doses of rifampin on the atogepant PK after
incorporation of the rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effect (observed in Study
CGP-PK-12). The simulation results showed that the reduction in hepatic uptake via inhibition of
OATP1B1 caused a significant increase in the atogepant AUC as compared to that without
considering the rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effect (AUCR 1.32 versus 0.44).
After incorporation of the rifampin-mediated possible BCRP induction effect (assumed to be the
same magnitude of induction as intestinal P-gp) in the model, the simulated AUCR (1.06) was
still not able to match the observed AUCR (0.41). Thus, the induction potential of rifampin on
OATP1B1/1B3 cannot be excluded and could explain, at least partially, the reduction in
atogepant exposure. The combined effect of rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition and
induction would not be expected to cause significant change in the systemic exposure of
atogepant as evidenced by the similar simulated AUCR (0.44) in the scenario without
considering rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition and induction effect to the observed
AUCR (0.41) (Table 118).

curves; Cmax, maximum concentration; CmaxR, ratio of maximum concentrations; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetics;

14.4.3. PBPK Model Application
The developed PBPK models were used to simulate the DDI for atogepant in the following
scenarios:
•

To predict the effect of a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducer on the PK
of atogepant if only considering CYP-mediated DDI in healthy subjects.
173

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

•

To predict the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., fluconazole) and a weak
CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., cimetidine) on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects.

•

To predict the PK of atogepant with and without complete knockout of P-gp or BCRP
transporter function in healthy subjects

Results
(1) Can atogepant PBPK models describe atogepant PK in healthy subjects?
Yes. The atogepant models were able to capture the observed atogepant PK following a single
oral dose (50, 60, 100, and 170 mg) and multiple oral dose administration (50, 100, and 170 mg
QD; 30 and 60 mg BID) of atogepant in healthy subjects (Figure 34 and Table 119). The
atogepant AUC was overpredicted following administration of 50 and 100 mg, presumably due
to the high variability in intestinal absorption.

(2) Can atogepant PBPK models predict the effect of a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong
CYP3A inducer on the atogepant PK if only considering CYP-mediated DDI in healthy
subjects?

175
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

Yes. The contribution of enzyme (CYP3A) and transporters (OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP)
in the disposition of atogepant were quantified using clinical PK and DDI data and sensitivity
analyses. The maximal contribution of CYP3A to the metabolism of atogepant has already been
assigned in the model (the contribution of CYP3A to the total metabolism of atogepant is 100%
and the contribution of CYP3A to the total clearance of atogepant is 75%). Thus, the possibility
of underestimation of atogepant DDI with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor or a strong CYP3A4
inducer may be lower. Please refer to Section 14.4.2 for details.
The model predicted an approximately 140% increase (AUC ratio of 2.43) in atogepant exposure
after multiple doses of itraconazole if only considering the CYP3A-mediated DDI effect in
healthy subjects. The model predicted an approximately 50% reduction (AUCR of 0.63) in
atogepant exposure after multiple doses of rifampin if only considering the rifampin-mediated
CYP3A4 induction effect in healthy subjects (Table 118).
(3) Can atogepant PBPK models predict the effect of fluconazole (a moderate CYP3A
inhibitor) and cimetidine (a weak CYP3A inhibitor) on the PK of atogepant in healthy
subjects?
Yes. The atogepant models are adequate to predict the effect of fluconazole and cimetidine on the
PK of atogepant in healthy subjects. About 69% increase in atogepant AUC was predicted with
fluconazole (loading dose of 400 mg followed by 200 mg QD), whereas the increase in atogepant
AUC(8.0%) with cimetidine (400 mg BID) was deemed clinically insignificant.
(4) Can atogepant PBPK models predict the PK of atogepant with or without complete
knockout of P-gp or BCRP transporter function in healthy subjects?
Yes. The model simulated atogepant exposure was increased about 20% if completely knocked
out of the intestinal and hepatic P-gp or intestinal and hepatic BCRP-mediated transport
processes, indicating low DDI potential of atogepant with a selective P-gp or BCRP inhibitor.
The prediction was consistent with the clinical finding that the atogepant AUC increased about
25% if coadministered with quinidine (a P-gp inhibitor) (Study 3101-103-002).

176
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4864103

NDA 215206
Qulipta (atogepant)

16. Efficacy: Additional Information and
Assessment
16.1. Sensitivity Analyses for Study CGP-MD-01
A pattern-mixture model based on nonignorable missing (i.e., missing not at random) was
conducted to assess the robustness of the primary mixed-effects model for repeated measures
analysis to the possible violation of the missing at random assumption. In the pattern-mixture
model, the placebo group was used as reference to impute missing values. Each imputed dataset
is analyzed using analysis of covariance with treatment group as a factor and baseline value as a
covariate and the estimates and p-values were obtained from combining all the results from each
individual analysis. The result of the pattern-mixture model is consistent with the primary
analysis (Table 121). As shown in Figure 35, the change in the number of migraine days after the
first month has similar trends in the placebo and atogepant groups. Therefore, imputing missing
data for the atogepant groups based on the pattern of the placebo group yielded a similar result as
the mixed-effects model for repeated measures analysis under missing at random assumption.

In study CGP-MD-01, it was required that a minimum of 12 days’ diary data during each
postbaseline 4-week treatment period be evaluable for that particular period. However, the
requirement was updated to “14 days” in study 3101-301-002 as requested by FDA during the
End of Phase 2 meeting. Therefore, the statistical reviewer conducted an additional analysis
using the “14 days” requirement for study CGP-MD-01. The estimated treatment effects were
similar to the primary analysis, although the p-value after adjusting for multiplicity is slightly
larger than 0.05 for the atogepant 60 mg QD group, and the atogepant 30 mg QD group (Table
123). The analysis using the evaluable requirement of “12 days” is considered as the primary
analysis as that was consistently specified in all versions of the Statistical Analysis Plan.

based on primary measures collected during the double-blind and follow-up periods. The result
was consistent with the primary analysis (Table 124).

16.3. Subgroup Analyses for Study CGP-MD-01
Atogepant groups showed consistent reductions from baseline in monthly migraine days when
compared with placebo across all subgroups of age, sex, and race, except for groups of small
sample size. The treatment effect appears to be larger for patients with prior exposure to a
migraine preventive medication.

20. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
OSI inspected the clinical sites of Robin Kroll, M.D. and Brock McConnehey, D.O. covering
Protocol CGP-MD-01 and Melvin Helm, M.D. and John Michael Hemphill, M.D. covering
Protocol 3101-301-002. All four clinical sites were in the United States. Despite some protocol
deviations noted at the sites of Drs. Kroll and McConnehey, the studies appear to have been
conducted adequately and the data generated by these sites appear acceptable in support of the
respective indication.

Qulipta (atogepant)

22. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
(1) A juvenile animal toxicology study of atogepant in rats.
(2) A randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled efficacy and safety study under the
PediatricResearch Equity Act for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in
children 6 through 17 years of age. This efficacy study must be designed to show
superiority of atogepant over placebo and is to be submitted as a special protocol
assessment (SPA). A pharmacokinetics evaluation needs to be conducted in children 6
through 11 years of age to determine the appropriate dosing in the efficacy portion of the
study in this age group.
(3) A study under the Pediatric Research Equity Act to evaluate the long-term safety of
preventive treatment of episodic migraine in patients 6 through 17 years of age. This
study should be a minimum of one year in length.
(4) A prospective pregnancy exposure registry cohort analyses in the United States that
compare the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women with migraine exposed to
atogepant during pregnancy with two unexposed control populations: one consisting of
women with migraine who have not been exposed to atogepant before and during
pregnancy and the other consisting of women without migraine. The registry will identify
and record pregnancy complications, major and minor congenital malformations,
spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, elective terminations, preterm births,
small-for-gestational-age births, and any other adverse outcomes, including postnatal
growth and development. Outcomes will be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant
outcomes, including effects on postnatal growth and development, will be assessed
through at least the first year of life.
(5) A pregnancy outcomes study using a different study design than provided for in the
pregnancy registry postmarketing requirement above (for example, a retrospective cohort
study using claims or electronic medical record data with outcome validation or a case
control study) to assess major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions,
stillbirths, preterm births, and small-for-gestational-age births in women exposed to
atogepant during pregnancy compared to an unexposed control population.
(6) A dose range-finding study of orally administered atogepant in female CD-1 mouse.
(7) A 2-year carcinogenicity study of orally administered Qulipta in female mouse.
Enhanced pharmacovigilance is recommended for liver toxicity, cardiovascular (including
hypertension), cerebrovascular, and serious gastrointestinal events.

There were three investigators in CGP-MD-01 who did not provide financial disclosure forms.
One site subsequently submitted the required forms. Two other sites did not submit forms, but
only two subjects were enrolled in the study during the time these investigators were active in the
study. The number of patients enrolled by these sites was minimal compared with the overall
number of patients in the study. Removing these sites did not affect the p-value on the primary
endpoint.

There were 54 sites noted to not have financial disclosure documents for some of the staff

information that is assessed as adequate. Each site was removed individually for the statistical
analysis of the primary endpoint, and no site when removed resulted in reducing the p-value for
this analysis such that it was not statistically significant in Study 3101-301-002.
There were several investigators with financial interests. Removing the individual sites, as stated
previously, did not affect the primary endpoint negatively.

Drug-induced liver injury (DILI)
Atogepant (calcitonin gene related peptide
receptor antagonist)
Migraine headache prevention
AbbVie, Inc
Division of Neurology 2
Daniel Ngembus, RPM
Viveca Livezey, MD

Aug 25, 2021

Context:
Atogepant is an oral small molecule antagonist of the calcitonin gene related peptide
(CGRP) receptor. It is under NDA review for the prevention of migraine headaches in
adults with more than 15 migraine days per month. Other agents in this class have had
problems with drug-induced liver injury (DILI). We are asked by the Division of
Neurology 2 (DN2) to review “patients with abnormal LFTs for potential liver toxicity”
associated with Atogepant with particular attention to a severe adverse event that
resulted in fatality. We are asked to give opinion on whether atogepant “carries a risk of
potential liver toxicity and, if so, how this may inform labeling.”
Executive Summary:
We do not see significant liver toxicity that threatens approval of this drug. There were
no cases that met Hy’s Law criteria in the registration trials in which over 1300 patients
were given Atogepant. Patients were treated for 12 weeks in the placebo-controlled
trials and up to 52 weeks in the long-term open label studies. There was no significant
imbalance in transaminase elevations between placebo and treatment arms. The fatal
case of particular concern did not involve DILI (see Section 4.6.2). Nevertheless, other
agents in this class have had significant liver injury issues in development, and there
were 5 cases of probable DILI without jaundice overall. All cases did well with holding
the Atogepant. We do not recommend monitoring for liver injury with blood tests, but
we suggest discussing potential for liver injury in the label because rare cases of severe
liver injury could arise in the larger post-market population. Avoidance of use in patients
with severe hepatic impairment should be considered. We have full recommendations
including comments for labeling in Section 5.0

Abbreviations:
ALP: alkaline phosphatase

1.0 Rationale:
1.1 Disease: Migraine headaches effect over 1 billion people worldwide with
substantial morbidity and disability. Persons aged 25 to 35 carry the highest
incidence rates with more women than men by a 2:1 ratio (23 versus 10 per
1000 person-years). 1, 2 Theories on pathophysiology have moved away from
the idea that vascular constriction leads to migraines. Nowadays, theory on
migraine pathophysiology is based on cortical spreading by a propagating
wave of neuronal and glial depolarization (cortical spreading depression of
Leao). 3 This wave helps to explain the migraine aura. This wave of
depolarization reaches the trigeminal vascular system which is felt to be
central to the subsequent pain and other manifestations of a migraine attack.
Stimulation of the trigeminal system leads to release of several neuropeptides
including calcitonin gene related peptide (CGRP). These peptides then lead
to neurogenic inflammation by vasodilation and protein extravasation. CGRP
and its receptor are widely expressed in the peripheral and central nervous
systems. CGRP may also play a role in pain transmission. It is increased in
cerebral spinal fluid and cranial circulation during migraine headaches and
can trigger migraine attacks.
Ashina M, et al. Lancet 397:1485-95 (2021)
Lyngberg AC, et al. Am J Epidemiol 161:1066-73 (2005)
3
Cutrer, FM. Pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, and diagnosis of migraine in adults. UpToDate.com
(accessed May 14, 2021)
1
2

2

Reference ID: 4848947

1.2 Mechanism of action: Because of CGRP’s significant role, it became a
primary therapeutic target, and blocking of the CGRP receptor has been
shown to prevent migraine headaches. Both small molecules and
monoclonal antibodies aimed at blocking the CGRP receptor are approved or
in development. Atogepant (AGPT) is a small molecule antagonist similar to
two agents approved for migraine prevention.
1.3 CGRP receptor antagonists and DILI risk: Telcagepant is in the same class as

AGPT. Telcagepant development was stopped by another sponsor due to liver injury
signals in Phase III studies, primarily when given daily for more than 7 days. 4
Elevated transaminases occurred at a rate too high and severe to warrant further
development. Similarly, development of another agent, MK-3207 was also stopped
due to liver injury concerns. The mechanism of liver injury for these agents is
postulated to be a combination of bile acid accumulation (transport inhibition) and
oxidative stress (reactive metabolite formation), and less so from mitochondrial
toxicity. 5 In vitro and in silico testing have led to development of other CGRP
receptor antagonists with less hepatotoxicity. Since the abandonment of

telcagepant and MK-3207, several agents in the same class have
successfully made it to market without significant liver injury concerns.
Telcagepant structure (Figure 1):
Figure 1

1.3.1 FDA approved oral, small molecule CGRP antagonists (year of
approval): rimegepant (2020) and ubrogepant (2019)
1.3.2 FDA approved parenteral, monoclonal antibody CGRP antagonists:
Eptinezumab (IV), Galcanezumab (SC), Fremanezumab (SC),
Erenumab (SC)

Because the sponsor is hoping to market AGPT in Japan, a Phase 1 RCT
specifically looking at PK in Japanese versus Caucasians was done in 2017
(Study 3101-101-002, Sq 18, 5.3.3.3). 40 Japanese and 10 Caucasians
enrolled. The Japanese were randomized 4:1 to varying exposures of AGPT
and placebo. There were no cases of transaminase elevations above 3x ULN
in any patient, Japanese or Caucasian (Clinical Study Report, page 251).
7.0 Addendum 2: After our review of the 120 Day Safety Report (Sq 17, SDN 17)
outlined in Section 6.0, an information request (IR) to assess potential increased
DILI risk in Japanese and/or Asians in general was sent by email on Jun 2, 2021
with 4 specific requests. The sponsor’s response came on Jun 7, 2021 (Seq 19,
SDN 19, 1.11.3). Of note, the response had safety data through May 26, 2021, so
the same data are also contained in the Safety Report submitted June 30, 2021 (Sq
22, SDN 22, 5.3.5.3).
7.1 Response Review Summary: In totality, the data in the sponsor’s response do
not warrant labeling a difference in liver injury risk for Japanese or Asians.
While the ongoing OLE study of 113 Japanese patients so far enrolled (3101306-002) indicates a higher rate of transaminase elevation than the overall
rate in this NDA’s ISS, no such increase is seen for Asians in the ISS nor the
RCT from which patients entered 3101-306-002. We acknowledge that the
number of Asians in the ISS is too low to draw conclusions. However, the
RCT had 248 Asians randomized across 3 arms (placebo, AGPT 30 mg bid,
17

Reference ID: 4848947

AGPT 60 mg/d) for 12 weeks of treatment. Only 1 of 248 Asians had
elevation in transaminases which was unlikely attributable to the IP. The RCT
remains blinded. At least 113 of those 248 were Japanese and enrolled in
3101-306-002. This OLE has no comparator arm. Therefore, we cannot
definitively assign an increased risk of DILI amongst Japanese, or Asians in
general, based on these data.
Similarly, we do not think labeling for a possible difference in DILI risk for
blacks is needed. There was a higher rate of transaminase elevations in
blacks on AGPT in the Group 2 ISS, but the number of blacks on placebo is
small at 38 and may not offer a large enough comparator arm. The number of
blacks in the Group 1 ISS was larger (69 placebo vs. 223 AGPT), and no
clear differences were seen in transaminase elevations amongst blacks.
Therefore, the data are equivocal.
Our detailed review follows (Section 7.2)
7.2 Sponsor Response Review:
7.2.1 Response to request 1: We had requested clarification whether the
ongoing, 52-week, open label study 3101-306-002 is restricted to
Japanese nationals. The sponsor replied that it is so restricted.
Therefore, the overall rates of enzyme elevations and DILI cases
for this study reflect Japanese nationality rates and can be
compared to other race or nationality groups in the ISS.
7.2.2 Response to request 2: We requested current enrollment status of
3101-306-002. The sponsor replied that 113 patients had enrolled
and included in the safety population as of May 26, 2021.
Therefore, we presume that all 113 have taken at least one dose of
AGPT at 60 mg/d. There is no comparator arm in this study.
7.2.3 Response to request 3: We requested elevation in liver test rates
for Study 3101-306-002 in tabular form. The sponsor sent the table
below. The rates of ALT and AST elevations are higher compared
to ISS Group 2 of 1198 patients also on 60 mg/d (see Section 4.5,
Table 4 above). For example, the rate for ALT or AST elevation >3x
and >5x ULN were 2.7% to 4.4% and 1.8% to 2.7% for Japanese
nationals, respectively. The rates of elevations in ALT or AST at
the same multiples of ULN were 1.4% and 0.7% for ISS Group 2.

Study 3101-303-002: In this blinded RCT, the rates of transaminase
elevations were low across all races. Only one Asian out of 248 had
an ALT 3-5x ULN. No unblinded data were provided. A narrative
was provided for the one Asian case. We assessed it as possible
to unlikely DILI due to AGPT, though if DILI is present than there
was partial tolerance with improvement in liver enzymes while still
on IP. Otherwise, fatty liver and autoimmune hepatitis compete
well. Transaminases rose to 2.6 to 3.5x ULN at day 14. Patient had
gained 3.2 kg from screening to day 14. IP was held on Day 16 and
20

Reference ID: 4848947

then restarted on Day 28. Transaminases fell to 1.0x to 1.7x ULN
through day 56 while still on IP. ANA was 1:40. Other serologic
evaluations were negative. Imaging showed fatty liver.

Summary

This review evaluates statistically the data of the 2-year oral carcinogenicity study of AGN241689 in Wistar Han Rats and 2-year oral carcinogenicity study in mice. The review analyzes
the dose-response relationship of tumor incidences and mortality (including tumor-related
mortality). The analyses results showed that AGN-241689 had no effects on survival or tumor
incidence in either species.

Rat Study: Rats (60/sex/group) were dosed by oral gavage with AGN-241689 once daily for
up to 104 weeks. The respective AGN-241689 doses in the water control (WC), vehicle control
(VC), low (LD), mid (MD), and high-dose (HD) groups were 0, 0, 10, 20, and 100 mg/kg/day
for male rats and 0, 0, 25, 65, and 200 mg/kg/day for female rats.
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in
mortality in both males and females. The respective survival rates in the WC, VC, LD, MD, or
HD of AGN-241689 at the time they were terminated were 72%, 75%, 70%, 70%, and 72% in
males and 62%, 63%, 72%, 62%, and 72% in females.
Tumor analysis results (Table 1) showed that the positive dose response was statistically
significant in the incidence of rare benign polyp tumor in the cervix in females when
compared with vehicle control group (0.0154 <0.025). For this tumor, the incidence rate of
any treated group was not statistically significantly greater than that of the vehicle control
group or water control group. For this tumor type in combined organ of cervix and uterus,
the positive dose response was not statistically significant.
Mouse Study: Mice (60/sex/group) were dosed by oral gavage with AGN-241689 once daily
for up to 104 weeks. The respective AGN-241689 doses in the water control (WC), vehicle
control (VC), low (LD), mid (MD), and high-dose (HD) groups were 0, 0, 5, 20, and 75
mg/kg/day for male mice and 0, 0, 5, 30, and 160 mg/kg/day for female mice.

The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in
mortality in both males and females. The respective survival rates in the WC, VC, LD, MD, or
HD of AGN-241689 at the time they were terminated were 47%, 50%, 62%, 53%, and 50% in
males and 48%, 48%, 57%, 55%, and 58% in females.
Tumor analysis results (Table 1) showed that the positive dose response was statistically
significant in the incidence of rare malignant rhabdomyosarcoma tumor in subcutis in male
mice (p=0.0164<0.025) when compared with water control group. For this tumor type, the
incidence rate of any treated group was not statistically significantly greater than that of the
vehicle control group or water control group.

Background

The sponsor, AbbVie Inc., is submitting, under section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug,
and Cosmetic Act and 21 CFR 314.50, a New Drug Application (NDA) for atogepant tablets,
10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg. The proposed indication is for the preventive treatment of migraine
in adults with < 15 migraine days per month. The sponsor submitted two carcinogenicity study
reports: Study cgp-tx-06-a1: “A 2-Year Carcinogenicity Study by Oral Gavage in Wistar Han
Rat” and Study cgp-tx-05-a1: “A 2-Year Carcinogenicity Study by Oral Gavage in the Mouse”
The study reports and the electronic SEND datasets were submitted on 1/28/2021 (via NDA
215206).
The phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component of the effect of
treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor incidence
rate as dose increases. Results of this review have been discussed with the nonclinical team.

3

Rat Study- cgp-tx-06-a1

Study Report:
SAS data:

cgp-tx-06-a1.pdf (statistical report on page 890)
SEND datasets

The objective of this study was to evaluate the carcinogenic potential of the test item, AGN241689, a calcitonin gene-related peptide receptor antagonist, when given by daily oral gavage
for a minimum of 104 consecutive weeks to Wistar Han Rats. The respective AGN-241689
doses in the water control (WC), vehicle control (VC), low (LD), mid (MD), high-dose (HD)
groups were displayed in following table:

Assessment of carcinogenicity was based on anatomic pathology to include incidences of
masses or tumors and possible effects on survival. Mortality, clinical observations, body
weight, and food consumption were also monitored and evaluated.

File Name: NDA215206Carcin_Primary_2.doc

Reference ID: 4840868

NDA 215206 ● Atogepant ● AbbVie/Allergan International LTD ● Carcinogenicity Study

3.1

Page 6 of 32

Sponsor’s Analyses
3.1.1

Survival Analysis

Intercurrent mortality data were analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product-limit method.
An overall test comparing all groups was conducted using a log-rank test12. Any animal with
accidental injury that caused death or unscheduled sacrifice was censored in the estimation.
In addition, all animals still alive at the end of the experimental period were censored at the
following day. If this overall test was significant (p <0.05) and there were more than two
groups, then a follow up analysis was done where each treatment group was compared to the
control group using a log-rank test. Results of all pair-wise comparisons are reported at the
0.05 and 0.01 significance levels. All endpoints were analyzed using two-tailed tests.
Sponsor’s concluded results: No AGN-241689-related causes of death occurred in either
sex in this study.
3.1.2

Tumor Data Analysis

The statistical evaluation of tumor data was limited to subcutis and hemolymphoreticular
tissue using all study animals, to all non-secondary neoplastic lesions found in Study Planrequired tissues/sites, and to the combination of hemangiosarcoma findings across whole
body.
Palpable neoplastic lesions found under skin, subcutis, and study plan-required glands were
statistically analyzed in a “mortality independent” context according to Peto’s onset rate
using all study animals. Whereas, non-palpable neoplastic findings were statistically analyzed
in a “mortality dependent” context according to Peto’s prevalence and death rate methods.
For each dataset of interest, a one-tailed Peto’s survival-adjusted trend test, using dose level
scores, was conducted in order to assess significance of tumor rate increase across vehicle
control group (Group 2) and test item treated groups. In addition, a two-tailed Peto’s was
used to compare the incidence rates of Group 2 and water control group (Group 1).
If the result revealed significant difference between Groups 1 and 2, then an upper-tailed
Peto's trend test was used in order to assess significance of tumor rate increase in each of the
test item treated groups when compared to each of Groups 1 and 2. Otherwise, an uppertailed Peto's trend test was used in order to assess significance of tumor rate increase in each
of the test item treated groups when only compared to Group 2.
As per Lin (Lin, K.K. 1997), the discrete permutation distribution tests for dose responses
were used for individual tumor types with 10 or less tumor-bearing animals. As
recommended by the FDA (Guidance for Industry, 2001), the overall trend and the pairwise
comparison tests were discussed in the main report according to the tumor prevalence
classification (common or rare).
The Peto (Peto R, Pike MC, Day NE et al, 1980) scoring system was used and assigned to
each neoplasm from all animals examined. Histopathology data was submitted separately in
an electronic format to Charles River Laboratories, Montreal, Canada for statistical analysis.
File Name: NDA215206Carcin_Primary_2.doc

Reference ID: 4840868

NDA 215206 ● Atogepant ● AbbVie/Allergan International LTD ● Carcinogenicity Study

Page 7 of 32

Results of the statistical analysis was provided to the study director for inclusion in the
toxicology report.
All p-values are reported using upper-tailed test, unless otherwise indicated. Evaluation
criteria (levels of significance) were applied differently for rare tumors (background rate of
1% or less) and common tumors (background rate greater than 1%).
Sponsor’s concluded results: There were no statistically significant differences between the
control and the AGN-241689 treated groups.
3.2

Reviewer’s Analyses

To verify the sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
pharmacologist, this review analyzed the SAS data sets of these studies received on 1/28/2021
via NDA 215206. The vehicle control group was compared to each of treated group and water
control group for the survival analyses and the tumor analyses.
3.2.1

Data Quality

The sponsor only submitted SEND database on 1/28/2021 via NDA 215206. The data quality
was acceptable.
3.2.2

Survival Analysis

The survival distributions of rates in all treatment groups were estimated using the KaplanMeier product limit method. For control, low, medium, and high dose groups, the dose
response relationship was tested using the likelihood ratio test and the homogeneity of survival
distributions were tested using the log-rank test. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rates are
given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. The
intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female
rats, respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of
survivals, are given in Tables 3A and 3B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percent) of death that
occurred prior to termination of the group were 17 (28%), 15 (25%), 18 (30%), 18 (30%), or 17
(28%) in male rats and 23 (38%), 22 (37%), 17 (28%), 23 (38%), or 17 (28%) in female rats in
the WC, VC, LD, MD, or HD groups, respectively. The survival analyses didn’t show any
statistically significant dose response relationship in mortality in males and females.
3.2.3

Tumor Data Analysis

The tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationships and pairwise comparisons of
control group with each of the treated groups. Both the dose response relationship tests and
pairwise comparisons were performed using the Poly-k method described in the papers of
File Name: NDA215206Carcin_Primary_2.doc

Reference ID: 4840868

NDA 215206 ● Atogepant ● AbbVie/Allergan International LTD ● Carcinogenicity Study

Page 8 of 32

Bailer and Portier [2] and Bieler and Williams [3]. In this method an animal that lives the full
study period ( wmax ) or dies before the terminal sacrifice but develops the tumor type being
tested gets a score of sh =1. An animal that dies at week wh without developing the tumor
k

before the end of the study gets a score of sh =  wh  <1. The adjusted group size is defined as
 wmax 

Σ sh . As an interpretation, an animal with score sh =1 can be considered as a whole animal
while an animal with score sh < 1 can be considered as a partial animal. The adjusted group size
Σ sh is equal to N (the original group size) if all animals live up to the end of the study or if each
animal that dies before the terminal sacrifice develops at least one tumor of the tumor type
being tested, otherwise the adjusted group size is less than N. These adjusted group sizes are
then used for the dose response relationship (or the pairwise) tests using the Cochran-Armitage
test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value of k, which depends
on the tumor incidence pattern with the increased dose. For long term 104-week standard rat
and mouse studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature. Hence, this reviewer used k=3
for the analysis of this data. For the calculation of p-values the exact permutation method was
used.
The adjusted levels of significance for testing a positive dose response in the 2-year rat study
are 0.005 and 0.025 for a common tumor and a rare tumor, respectively. The adjusted levels
of significance for the pairwise comparison in the 2-year rat study are 0.01 and 0.05 for a
common tumor and a rare tumor, respectively. A rare tumor is defined as one in which the
tumor rate is less than 1% in the vehicle control group. The tumor rates and the p-values of
the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 5A, and 5B in the appendix for male and female
rats, respectively.
This reviewer also performed a separate set of tumor data analyses to test the positive dose
responses in tumor incidence rates among the water control group and the low, medium, and
high groups, and to test the pairwise comparisons between the water control group and each of
the low, medium, high, and vehicle control groups. Since there were no statistically significant
positive responses, and statistically significant differences in tumor incidences between the
water control group and the vehicle control group in all the tumor types tested, the results of
this separate set of tumor data analyses were not included in this reviewer’s report.
Reviewer’s findings: Following table displays the tumor types showing p-values less than
or equal to 0.05 either for dose response relationships or for pairwise comparisons of treated
groups and control.

for a minimum of 104 consecutive weeks to mice. The respective AGN-241689 doses in the
water control (WC), vehicle control (VC), low (LD), mid (MD), high-dose (HD) groups were

Assessment of toxicity was based on dose analysis, morbidity, mortality, injury, body weight,

4.1

Sponsor’s Analyses
4.1.1

Survival Analysis

The sponsor used the same survival analysis methods for the rat study in this mouse study.
Sponsor’s concluded results: AGN-241689 administered orally to mice for up to 104
weeks had no effect on survival.
4.1.2

Tumor Data Analysis

The sponsor used the same tumor data analysis methods for the rat study in this mouse
study.
Sponsor’s findings: There were no neoplastic findings attributed to AGN-241689.

4.2

Reviewer’s Analyses

To verify the sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
pharmacologist, this review analyzed the SAS data sets of these studies received on 1/28/2021
via NDA 215206. The vehicle control group was compared to each of treated group and water
control group for the survival analyses and the tumor analyses.
4.2.1

Data Quality

The sponsor only submitted SEND database on 1/28/2021 via NDA 215206. The data quality
was acceptable.
4.2.2

Survival Analysis

The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rates of all treatment groups are given in Figures 2A and
2B in the appendix for male and female mice, respectively. The intercurrent mortality data of all
treatment groups are given in Tables 2A and 2B in the appendix for male and female mice,
respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals
for control, low, medium, and high dose groups are given in Tables 4A and 4B in the appendix
for male and female mice, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percent) of death that

occurred prior to termination of the group were 32 (53%), 30 (50%), 23 (38%), 28 (47%), or 30

4.2.3

Tumor Data Analysis

The tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationships and pairwise comparisons of the

the low, medium, high, and vehicle control groups. Only one tumor type (listed in the following
table) of rare malignant rhabdomyosarcoma tumor in subcutis in male mice had a statistically
significant positive responses between the water control group and treated group. The results of
this separate set of tumor data analyses were not included in this reviewer’s report.

Based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing discussed above, the positive dose
response was statistically significant in the incidence of rare malignant rhabdomyosarcoma
tumor in subcutis in males (p=0.0164<0.025) when compared with water control group.
For this tumor type, the incidence rate of any treated group was not statistically significantly
greater than that of the vehicle control group or water control group.

Page 12 of 32

Conclusion

This review evaluates statistically the data of the 2-year oral carcinogenicity study of AGN241689 in Wistar Han Rats and 2-year oral carcinogenicity study in mice. The review analyzes
the dose-response relationship of tumor incidences and mortality (including tumor-related
mortality). The analyses results showed that AGN-241689 had no effects on survival or tumor
incidence in either species.

& X/ZZ (YY): X=number of tumor bearing animals; YY=mortality weighted total number of animals; ZZ=unweighted total
number of animals observed; NS = Not significant. Note: The p-values marked with an asterisk * indicate statistically significant
dose responses at 0.005 and 0.025 for a common tumor and a rare tumor, respectively.

Rat Study: Rats (60/sex/group) were dosed by oral gavage with AGN-241689 once daily for
up to 104 weeks. The respective AGN-241689 doses in the water control (WC), vehicle control
(VC), low (LD), mid (MD), and high-dose (HD) groups were 0, 0, 10, 20, and 100 mg/kg/day
for male rats and 0, 0, 25, 65, and 200 mg/kg/day for female rats.
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in
mortality in both males and females. The respective survival rates in the WC, VC, LD, MD, or
HD of AGN-241689 at the time they were terminated were 72%, 75%, 70%, 70%, and 72% in
males and 62%, 63%, 72%, 62%, and 72% in females.
Tumor analysis results (Table 1) showed that the positive dose response was statistically
significant in the incidence of rare benign polyp tumor in the cervix in females when
compared with vehicle control group (0.0154 <0.025). For this tumor, the incidence rate of
any treated group was not statistically significantly greater than that of the vehicle control
group or water control group. For this tumor type in combined organ of cervix and uterus,
the positive dose response was not statistically significant.
Mouse Study: Mice (60/sex/group) were dosed by oral gavage with AGN-241689 once daily
for up to 104 weeks. The respective AGN-241689 doses in the water control (WC), vehicle
File Name: NDA215206Carcin_Primary_2.doc

Reference ID: 4840868

NDA 215206 ● Atogepant ● AbbVie/Allergan International LTD ● Carcinogenicity Study

Page 13 of 32

control (VC), low (LD), mid (MD), and high-dose (HD) groups were 0, 0, 5, 20, and 75
mg/kg/day for male mice and 0, 0, 5, 30, and 160 mg/kg/day for female mice.
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in
mortality in both males and females. The respective survival rates in the WC, VC, LD, MD, or
HD of AGN-241689 at the time they were terminated were 47%, 50%, 62%, 53%, and 50% in
males and 48%, 48%, 57%, 55%, and 58% in females.
Tumor analysis results (Table 1) showed that the positive dose response was statistically
significant in the incidence of rare malignant rhabdomyosarcoma tumor in subcutis in male
mice (p=0.0164<0.025) when compared with water control group. For this tumor type, the
incidence rate of any treated group was not statistically significantly greater than that of the
vehicle control group or water control group.

Reference ID: 4840868

Clinical Review Memorandum
Division of Hepatology and Nutrition, Drug-Induced Liver Injury (DILI) Team
Office of New Drugs
Center for Drug Evaluation and Research
Food and Drug Administration
NDA
Drug name
Sponsor
Review item
Requesting Division
Author name
Date
I.

215206
Atogepant
AbbVie, Inc
IR Response (SDN 19, SN 19)
Division of Neurology-2 (DN2)
Paul H. Hayashi, MD, MPH
Jul 7, 2021

OVERALL ASSESSMENT AND RECOMMENDATION(S)
The response data provided by the sponsor does not warrant labeling a
difference in liver injury risk for Japanese, or Asians overall. While the ongoing
open-label extension (OLE) study (3101-306-002) indicates a higher rate of
transaminase elevation than the overall rate in the NDA’s Integrated Summary of
Safety (ISS), no such increase is seen for Asians in the ISS nor the randomized
clinical trial (RCT) from which patients entered 3101-306-002. The rates of
transaminase elevations in the OLE are 2.7% for 5x ULN and 1% for >10-20x
ULN without any cases of hyperbilirubinemia. The number of Asians in the ISS is
too low to draw conclusions. However, the RCT had 248 Asians with only 1
patient having elevation in transaminases which was unlikely attributable to the
investigational product (IP). The RCT remains blinded. The OLE has no
comparator arm. Therefore, we cannot definitively assign an increased risk of
DILI amongst Japanese, or Asians in general, based on these data.

II.

BACKGROUND

The DILI team has been helping DN2 with assessing DILI risk in this NDA since
February 18, 2021. Atogepant is an oral small molecule antagonist of the
calcitonin gene related peptide (CGRP) receptor. It is under NDA review for the
prevention of migraine headaches in adults. Other agents in this class have had
problems with drug-induced liver injury (DILI). We were asked by the Division of
Neurology 2 (DN2) to review “patients with abnormal LFTs for potential liver
toxicity” associated with Atogepant with particular attention to a severe adverse
event that resulted in fatality. We werw asked to give opinion on whether
investigational product (IP) “carries a risk of potential liver toxicity and, if so, how
this may inform labeling.”
Our full written consult is in process, but thus far we have not seen significant
liver toxicity potential that threatens approval. At our suggestion, an IR was sent
1

Reference ID: 4822400

on Jun 2, 2021 asking for more information about potential increased risk of DILI
amongst Japanese in a trial outside the NDA’s ISS. The response arrived on Jun
7, 2021 (SDN 19, SN 19). Of note, the response had safety data through May 26,
2021, so the same data are contained in the Safety Report submitted June 30,
2021 (Sq 22, SDN 22, 5.3.5.3).
III. SIGNIFICANT REVIEW FINDINGS
Review of Sponsor Response:
a. Response to request 1: We had requested clarification whether the
ongoing, 52-week, open label study 3101-306-002 is restricted to
Japanese nationals. The sponsor replied that it is so restricted.
Therefore, the overall rates of enzyme elevations and DILI cases
for this study reflect Japanese nationality rates and can be
compared to other race or nationality groups in the ISS.
b. Response to request 2: We requested current enrollment status of
3101-306-002. The sponsor replied that 113 patients had enrolled
and included in the safety population as of May 26, 2021.
Therefore, we presume that all 113 have taken at least one dose of
AGPT at 60 mg/d. There is no comparator arm in this study.
c. Response to request 3: We requested elevation in liver test rates
for Study 3101-306-002 in tabular form. See Table 1. The rates of
ALT and AST elevations are higher compared to ISS Group 2 of
1198 patients also on 60 mg/d (see Section 4.5, Table 4 above).
For example, the rate for ALT or AST elevation >3x and >5x ULN
were 2.7% to 4.4% and 1.8% to 2.7% for Japanese nationals,
respectively. The rates of elevations in ALT or AST at the same
multiples of ULN were 1.4% and 0.7% for ISS Group 2.

Study 3101-303-002: In this blinded RCT, the rates of transaminase
elevations were low across all races. Only one Asian out of 248 had
an ALT 3-5x ULN. No unblinded data were provided. A narrative
was provided for the one Asian case. We assessed it as possible to
unlikelyDILI due to AGPT, though if DILI is present than there was
partial tolerance with improvement in liver enzymes while still on IP.
Otherwise, fatty liver and autoimmune hepatitis compete well.
Transaminases rose to 2.6 to 3.5x ULN at day 14. Patient had
gained 3.2 kg from screening to day 14. IP was held on Day 16 and
then restarted on Day 28. Transaminases fell to 1.0x to 1.7x ULN
through day 56 while still on IP. ANA was 1:40. Other serologic
evaluations were negative. Imaging showed fatty liver.
IV.

CONCLUSIONS

The data in the sponsor’s response do not warrant labeling a difference in liver
injury risk for Japanese or Asians. While the ongoing OLE study of 113 Japanese
patients so far enrolled (3101-306-002) indicates a higher rate of transaminase
elevation than the overall rate in this NDA’s ISS, no such increase is seen for
Asians in the ISS nor the RCT from which patients entered 3101-306-002. We
acknowledge that the number of Asians in the ISS is too low to draw conclusions.
However, the RCT had 248 Asians randomized across 3 arms (placebo, AGPT
30 mg bid, AGPT 60 mg/d) for 12 weeks of treatment. Only 1 of 248 Asians had
elevation in transaminases which was unlikely attributable to the IP. The RCT
remains blinded. At least 113 of those 248 were Japanese and enrolled in 3101306-002. This OLE has no comparator arm. The rates of transaminase
elevations in the OLE are 2.7% for 5x ULN and 1% for >20x ULN without any
cases of hyperbilirubinemia. Therefore, we cannot definitively assign an
increased risk of DILI for Japanese, or Asians, based on these data.
Similarly, we cannot assign a difference in DILI risk for blacks. There was a
higher rate of transaminase elevations in blacks on AGPT in the Group 2 ISS, but
the number of blacks on placebo is small at 38 and may not offer a large enough
comparator arm. The number of blacks in the Group 1 ISS was larger (69
placebo vs. 223 AGPT), and no clear differences were seen in transaminase
elevations amongst blacks. Therefore, the data are equivocal.
V.

RECOMMENDED REGULATORY ACTION(S)

We do not recommend any regulatory action for this IR response currently. We
have informed DN2 of our assessment. We will continue to collaborate with them
during their NDA review. Our full written consult assessing DILI risk in this NDA
is pending our further review and communication with DN2.

I.

OVERALL ASSESSMENT AND RECOMMENDATION(S)
This 120-day Safety Update Report included 4 new cases of elevation in
transaminases. We assessed 3 as unlikely and 1 as possible DILI due to
investigational product (IP). None had hyper bilirubinemia. The possible DILI
case is still on IP with monitoring of improved transaminases. No new cases had
transaminase elevations over 8x ULN. These data do not significantly change
our ongoing assessment of this NDA’s DILI risk. We have no recommendations
for this report at this time.

II.

BACKGROUND

The DILI team has been helping DN2 with assessing DILI risk in this NDA since
February 18, 2021. Atogepant is an oral small molecule antagonist of the
calcitonin gene related peptide (CGRP) receptor. It is under NDA review for the
prevention of migraine headaches in adults. Other agents in this class have had
problems with drug-induced liver injury (DILI). We were asked by the Division of
Neurology 2 (DN2) to review “patients with abnormal LFTs for potential liver
toxicity” associated with Atogepant with particular attention to a severe adverse
event that resulted in fatality. We were asked to give opinion on whether
investigational product (IP) “carries a risk of potential liver toxicity and, if so, how
this may inform labeling.”
Our full written consult is in process, but thus far we have not seen significant
liver toxicity potential that threatens approval. Much of the data in this Safety
Report submitted June 30, 2021 (Sq 22, SDN 22, 5.3.5.3) appear in the Jun 7,
2021 (SDN 19, SN 19) response to information request (IR), for which we
submitted a review also on July 7, 2021. Our full written consultation for
regarding DILI risk for this NDA is pending.
